Khovanov–Rozansky Homology of Coxeter Knots and Schröder Polynomials for paths under any line
Carmen Caprau, Nicolle González, Matthew Hogancamp, Mikhail Mazin

Abstract
. We introduce a family of generalized Schröder polynomials S τ ​ ( q , t , a ) S_{\tau}(q,t,a) , indexed by triangular partitions τ \tau and prove that S τ ​ ( q , t , a ) S_{\tau}(q,t,a) agrees with the Poincaré series of the triply graded Khovanov–Rozansky homology of the Coxeter knot K τ K_{\tau} associated to τ \tau . For all integers m , n , d ≥ 1 m,n,d\geq 1 with m , n m,n relatively prime, the ( d , m ​ n ​ d + 1 ) (d,mnd+1) -cable of the torus knot T ⁡ ( m , n ) T(m,n) appears as a special case. It is known that these knots are algebraic, and as a result we obtain a proof of the q = 1 q=1 specialization of the Oblomkov–Rasmussen–Shende conjecture for these knots. Finally, we show that our Schröder polynomial computes the hook components in the Schur expansion of the symmetric function appearing in the shuffle theorem under any line, thus proving a triangular version of the ( q , t ) (q,t) -Schröder theorem.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 57K18, 05E05; Secondary 05A15, 05A17, 05A19

1.
Introduction

Triply graded Khovanov–Rozansky homology is a rich invariant of knots and links, which categorifies the HOMFLY-PT polynomial. During the last fifteen years a myriad of conjectures have appeared relating the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of various special families of links to Catalan combinatorics
[
23
,
18
,
19
]
, the Hilbert schemes of points on a plane curve singularity
[
52
]
, representations of rational DAHA
[
30
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
, sheaves on the Hilbert scheme of points in the plane
[
28
,
29
]
, and refined Chern-Simmons theory
[
1
]
(see §
1.3
for brief summary of these conjectures).
In order to employ a
compute-both-sides
approach to such conjectures, one must first overcome the obvious challenge of computing Khovanov–Rozansky homology of the links or knots in question.

In
[
15
]
, the third named author and Ben Elias introduced a technique for computing Khovanov–Rozansky homology relying on a recursively defined polynomial
R_{\bf u,v}
for certain binary sequences
\bf u
and
\bf v
, with the torus link
T(n,n)
being the main example. This technique was then exploited in subsequent papers by Elias, Hogancamp, and Mellit to compute the homologies of various families of links: the paper
[
38
]
computes the homologies of
T(m,md)
and
T(m,md\pm 1)
for
m,d\geq 1
, followed shortly thereafter by
[
49
]
which computes the homology of
T(m,n)
for
m,n\geq 1
coprime, followed by
[
39
]
, generalizing all the aforementioned, which computes the homology of
T(m,n)
for
m,n\geq 1
, not necessarily coprime. Importantly for us, the aforementioned computations include some non-torus-links as well.

The initial motivation for this paper stemmed from the problem of computing and giving a combinatorial interpretation of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of iterated torus knots of the form
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
, where
m,n,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
m,n
are coprime. Along the way, we realized such knots are special cases of the knots whose homology was computed in
[
39
]
, which in turn coincide with a certain subfamily of
Coxeter knots

K_{\tau}
, where
\tau
is a
triangular partition
. Coxeter knots were previously studied in
[
53
,
19
]
and triangular partitions in
[
5
]
.

In this paper we investigate the Khovanov–Rozanksy homology of such knots. We introduce the new family of
triangular Schröder polynomials

S_{\tau}(q,t,a)
which we prove compute the Khovanov–Rozanksy homology of Coxeter knots
K_{\tau}
for triangular partitions. As a consequence of the knots
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
with
m,n
coprime being algebraic, we obtain a proof of a conjecture of Oblomkov–Rasmussen–Shende restricted to these knots in the special case when
q=1
. Combinatorially, our
triangular Schröder polynomials fit nicely into the existing
(q,t)
-Catalan combinatorics
[
20
,
4
,
35
,
36
,
31
,
34
,
32
,
23
,
3
,
48
,
9
,
6
]
. They generalize both the classical
(q,t)
-Catalan
[
20
]
and Schröder polynomials
[
13
]
and correspond to the hook components of the Schur expansion of the shuffle theorem under any line of Blasiak-Haiman-Morse-Pun-Seelinger
[
6
]
, thus providing a triangular generalization of the classical
(q,t)
-Schröder Theorem
of Haglund
[
31
]
.

1.1.
Triangular Schröder Polynomials and Invariant Subsets

Deeply ingrained in the many conjectures surrounding Khovanov–Rozansky homology is the combinatorics of Dyck paths and related
(q,t)
-Catalan objects. The
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomials were introduced in
[
20
]
and studied extensively in Haglund’s book
[
32
]
. In order to remain consistent with the notation used throughout this paper, we denote the
(q,t)
-Catalan by
C_{n,n+1}(q,t)
, where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

These polynomials appear in a wide variety of contexts. First,
C_{n,n+1}(q,t)
admits a combinatorial definition as a generating function which counts Dyck paths in the
n\times(n+1)
rectangle (which are certain lattice paths below the line
(n+1)y+nx=n(n+1)
consisting of north and west steps), weighted by some explicit combinatorially defined statistics
\mathtt{area}
and
\mathtt{dinv}
. In representation theory,
C_{n,n+1}(q,t)
appears as the coefficient of the Schur function
s_{(1^{n})}
in the Schur expansion of
\nabla e_{n}
, (which through work of Haiman
[
35
,
36
]
, coincides with the Frobenius character of the ring of a diagonal coinvariants). Here,
e_{n}
is the
n^{\text{th}}
elementary symmetric function and
\nabla
is the Bergeron-Garsia operator on symmetric functions
[
4
]
, which acts diagonally on modified Macdonald polynomials. In topology,
C_{n,n+1}(q,t)
also appears as the graded dimension of the lowest
a
-degree component of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of the
(n,n+1)
-torus knot (this was conjectured by Gorsky in
[
23
]
, and proven in
[
38
]
).

We now point out two directions in which
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomials have been generalized. In the first, one replaces the Dyck paths appearing in the definition of
C_{n,n+1}(q,t)
with the more general
Schröder paths

[
59
]
. These are certain lattice paths which are allowed to contain some number of northwest steps in addition to the usual north or west steps of a Dyck path. The resulting generating function
S_{n,n+1}(q,t,a)
is called the
Schröder polynomial

[
13
]
, defined so that the coefficient of
a^{k}
in
S_{n,n+1}(q,t,a)
is a generating function counting Schröder paths in the
n\times(n+1)
rectangle with exactly
k
diagonal steps. At the level of symmetric functions, this amounts to extracting the coefficient of hook-indexed Schur function
s_{(k,1^{n-k})}
(for a precise statement see §
1.4
); at the level of link homology, this amounts to taking the graded dimension of the higher
a
-degree components of Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
T(n,n+1)
.

The second direction in which we can generalize
C_{n,n+1}(q,t)
is to allow more Dyck paths in rectangles of arbitrary sizes. For instance, for a relatively prime pair of integers
m,n\geq 1
, one has the associated
rational
(q,t)
-Catalan

C_{m,n}(q,t)
which counts Dyck paths in an
m\times n
rectangle, weighted by an analogous pair of statistics
\mathtt{area}
,
\mathtt{dinv}
. As in the classical case,
C_{m,n}(q,t)
is indeed the sign component of an associated symmetric function, in this case defined by Hikita
[
37
]
. At the level of link homology,
C_{m,n}(q,t)
is the graded dimension of the lowest
a
-degree component of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
T(m,n)
(this was conjectured in
[
10
,
28
]
, and proven by Mellit in
[
49
]
). Of course, one can generalize in both directions, obtaining
rational Schröder polynomial

S_{m,n}(q,t,a)
which calculates the hook components of Hikita’s symmetric function and also the graded dimension of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
T(m,n)
in all
a
-degrees.

In
[
6
]
Blasiak–Haiman–Morse–Pun–Seelinger provide a further generalization in the aforementioned second direction by considering set of Dyck paths “under any line”. More precisely given arbitrary real numbers
r,s>0
, a
Dyck path under
L_{r,s}
is a path in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
consisting of unit length west and north steps, from
(\lfloor r\rfloor,0)
to
(0,\lfloor s\rfloor)
, and staying weakly below
L_{r,s}
. In
loc. cit.
it is shown how to construct an analogue of the Hikita symmetric function from the set of Dyck paths under
L_{r,s}
, and a combinatorial interpretation of the associated
(q,t)
-Catalan is given. From this perspective, the present paper provides the missing Schröder polynomial and link homology interpretation for paths “under any line”. In order to delve further into what this means, we must first introduce the notion of triangular partitions.

Given a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})
with
\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{k}\geq 0
, we identify
\lambda
with its partition diagram in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and index any cell
\Box\in\lambda
by its northeast coordinate
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Thus a partition can be viewed as a subset
\lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Any pair of positive real numbers
r,s>0
determines a partition
\tau_{r,s}
by declaring that the cell
\Box^{x,y}
with northeast corner at
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
is in
\tau_{r,s}
if and only if
ry+sx\leq rs
. In other words,
\tau_{r,s}
consists of all boxes contained between the line
L_{r,s}:=\{ry+sx=rs\}
and the coordinate axes. A
triangular partition
is any partition of the form
\tau_{r,s}
for some real numbers
r,s>0
. The combinatorics of triangular partitions were studied by Bergeron and the fourth named author in
[
5
]
, and are the underlying indexing object in the generalized shuffle theorem of Blasiak–Haiman–Morse–Pun–Seelinger
[
6
]
.

Observe that if
\pi
is a Dyck path under the line
L_{r,s}
then there is an associated partition
\lambda
consisting of all cells in the first quadrant and below
\pi
. By definition,
\lambda
will be a subpartition of
\tau_{r,s}
. This correspondence is a canonical bijection between Dyck paths under
L_{r,s}
and subpartitions of
\tau_{r,s}
. In light of this observation, a pair
(\lambda,\tau)
consisting of a triangular partition
\tau
and a subpartition
\lambda\subset\tau
will be referred to as a
\tau
-Dyck path
. As matter of notation, given real numbers
r,s>0
we will write
\mathrm{D}(r,s)
for the set of all
\tau_{r,s}
-Dyck paths.

Our definition of the Schröder polynomial associated to a triangular partition will utilize the recursions introduced by Gorsky-Mazin-Vazirani
[
26
,
27
]
and thus will be phrased in the language of invariant subsets rather than lattice paths, which we now briefly recall.

For coprime positive integers
m,n
, a set
\Delta\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is
0-normalized and
(m,n)
-invariant
if it contains
0
and is closed under translations by
+m
and
+n
, i.e.
\Delta+m,\Delta+n\subset\Delta
. Denote by
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
the set of all such subsets. In
[
26
,
27
]
, the fourth named author alongside Gorsky and Vazirani defined a bijection

\mathcal{A}:\mathrm{D}(m,n)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}\qquad\text{sending}\qquad\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})\mapsto\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\mathrm{Gaps}(\pi),

where
\mathrm{Gaps}(\pi):=\{mn-my-nx\;|\;(x,y)\in\tau_{m,n}\setminus\lambda\}
.
More generally, Gorsky–Mazin–Vazirani also considered invariant subsets indexed by certain trinary sequences
\bf x,y
in letters
\{0,1,\bullet\}
and defined the polynomial

Q_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(q,t,a):=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}}q^{\mathtt{area}^{\prime}(\Delta)}t^{-\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\Delta)}\prod_{k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\Delta)}(1+at^{-\xi_{k}(\Delta)}),

where
\mathtt{area},\mathtt{codinv}
, and
\mathtt{cogen}
are certain combinatorial statistics.
In particular, they proved that
Q_{\bf x,y}
satisfies recursion relations that coincide with those of Hogancamp-Mellit
[
39
]
, so that if
\bf u,v
are the binary sequences obtained from
\bf x,y
by omitting the bullets
\bullet
, then
Q_{\bf x,y}=R_{\bf u,v}

[
26
]
. As a consequence, they showed that the
(q,t)
-Schröder (and consequently Catalan) polynomial
S_{m,n}(q,t,a)
could be obtained directly as a certain
q,t,a
-graded sum over
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
.

In order to extend their construction to the setting of
\mathrm{D}(r,s)
with
r,s
positive real numbers, we choose an embedding of
\tau_{r,s}
into a larger triangular partition
\tau_{m,n}
, such that
m,n
are coprime positive integers with
L_{m,n}
parallel to
L_{r,s}
. Indeed, such a choice exists since any given triangular partition
\tau
may be cut out by lines of various slopes
[
5
]
. In particular, if
\tau_{r,s}\subset\tau_{m,n}
, then there exists
0<z\leq mn
such that
(r,s)=(z/n,z/m)
, for which if
\ell=\lfloor z\rfloor
then
\tau_{r,s}=\tau_{\ell/n,\ell/m}
and
\mathrm{D}(r,s)=\mathrm{D}(\ell/n,\ell/m)
. Since the choice of
\tau_{m,n}
plays a role in the definitions, we modify the notation slightly in order to make this choice explicit and set,

\tau_{m,n,\ell}:=\tau_{\ell/n,\ell/m},\qquad\qquad\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell):=\mathrm{D}(\ell/n,\ell/m),\qquad\text{and}\qquad L_{m,n,\ell}:=L_{\ell/n,\ell/m}.

So then, for any such triple
(m,n,\ell)
we define

\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}:=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}\;|\;\Delta\cap\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{m,n,l},\tau_{m,n})=\emptyset\},

and prove that

\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf x}(m,n,\ell),{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)}=(\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}-(mn-\ell)+(n+m))\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

(1.1)

where
{\bf x}(m,n,\ell),{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)
are certain trinary sequences constructed directly from the combinatorial data of
L_{m,n,\ell}
.

The
triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial
associated to the triple
(m,n,\ell)
is defined by

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a):=t^{|\tau_{m,n,\ell}|}Q_{{\bf x}(m,n,\ell),{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)}(q,t,a).

As before, we have a bijection

\mathcal{D}:\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}
, allowing us to give a formulation of these polynomials in terms of Schröder paths (see Theorem
4.26
).

Theorem A
.

We have

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)=\sum_{k\geq 0}a^{k}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)\\
|L|=k\end{subarray}}t^{-\sum_{\Box\in L}\xi(\pi,\Box)}\right),

(1.2)

where
\mathtt{AB}(\pi)
, with
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})
, is the set of all addable boxes of
\lambda
and
\mathtt{area}
,
\mathtt{dinv}
, and
\xi
are some explicit statistics.

In the language of lattice paths, an addable box of
\lambda
corresponds to a west step (W) immediately followed by a north step (N) (reading the path right to left and bottom to top). Thus, we can form a Schröder path by replacing each such pair (W,N) in a given subset
L\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)
of addable boxes with a single northwest pointing diagonal step. In particular, for each fixed
k
, the sum over pairs
(\pi,L)
can be interpreted as a sum over lattice paths
\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
with exactly
k
diagonal steps. Consequently, we recover the more familiar rational
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n}(q,t,a)
in the special case
\ell=mn-1
.

The following result, exhibited in Corollary
4.20
, is now immediate (see §
4
for details).

Theorem B
.

The triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
specializes to the triangular
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomial of
[
5
,
6
]
at
a=0
,

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,0)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}=:C_{m,n,\ell}(q,t).

1.2.
The Coxeter Knot Associated to a Partition

The family of (positive) Coxeter knots was introduced in the work of Oblomkov and Rozansky
[
53
]
and was recently proved to coincide with the family of monotone knots independently defined by Galashin–Lam
[
19
]
. Coxeter knots featured prominently in connections between link homology and the geometry of the Hilbert scheme of points on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
(see also
[
29
]
). We recall their construction next.

Given an integer
M\geq 1
, let
\mathrm{Br}_{M}
denote the braid group on
M
strands with
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{M-1}
the usual Artin generators.
Let
\lambda
be a partition with
\lambda_{1}<M
, and let
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{M}=0
be the lengths of the columns of
\lambda
, i.e.
\mu
is the transpose partition of
\lambda
, extending by zeros to obtain a sequence of length
M
. We define the
Coxeter knot associated to
\lambda
, denoted
K_{\lambda}
, to be the closure of the
M
-strand braid

\beta^{cox}_{M,\lambda}:=\mathrm{JM}_{M,2}^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}\mathrm{JM}_{M,3}^{\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}}\dots\mathrm{JM}_{M,M}^{\mu_{M-1}-\mu_{M}}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{M-1},

where
\mathrm{JM}_{M,i}\in\mathrm{Br}_{M}
is the
M
-strand
Jucys-Murphy
braid

\mathrm{JM}_{M,i}=(\sigma_{M-i+1}\sigma_{M-i+2}\cdots\sigma_{M-1})(\sigma_{M-1}\cdots\sigma_{M-i+2}\sigma_{M-i+1})

(these Jucys-Murphy braids are obtained from the more standard ones
(\sigma_{i-1}\cdots\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{i-1})
by conjugating with the half-twist).
It is straightforward to prove (see Proposition
5.2
) that the closure of
\beta^{cox}_{M,\lambda}
depends only on
\lambda
(and not on
M
) up to isotopy.

We are particularly interested in knots of the form
K_{\tau}
where
\tau
is a triangular partition. A few important examples include the following:

(1)

If
\tau=\tau_{m,n}
with
m,n\geq 1
coprime integers, then
K_{\tau}=T(n,m)
. This is most easily seen by translating into the language of monotone knots using a result of Galashin–Lam
[
19
]
.

(2)

If
\tau=\tau_{M,N}
with
M,N\geq 1
arbitrary positive integers (not necessarily coprime), then
K_{\tau}
is a certain cabled torus knot. Specifically let
d=\gcd(M,N)
and write
(M,N)=(md,nd)
, so that
m,n\geq 1
are coprime. Then
K_{\tau}=T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
, where
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
is the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of the
(m,n)
-torus knot,
T(m,n)
. This fact was observed in Galashin–Lam
[
18
]
, and we give an alternate proof in Appendix
B
.

To compute the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
K_{\tau}
, we will recognize that the family of knots
K_{\tau}
(in which
\tau
is a triangular partition) coincides with the family of knots whose homology is already computed by Hogancamp and Mellit in
[
39
]
. Let us recall this latter family now.

Given
\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
binary sequences with exactly one ‘1’, we write
\mathbf{u}=(0^{i}10^{j})
and
\mathbf{v}=(0^{k}10^{l})
. Denote by
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
the closure of the braid

(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{i})(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{m-1})^{k}(\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{m-1})^{l}.

For an example diagram of such a link, see Figure
5
. The main result of
[
39
]
is a computation of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
, via a recursion involving general pairs of binary sequences with the same number of occurences of ‘1’. Specifically,
[
39
]
defines series
R_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}(q,t,a)
indexed by pairs of binary sequences
\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
with
\sum_{i}u_{i}=\sum_{j}v_{j}
, and proves that

(1-q)(at^{-1/2}q^{-1/2})^{-\delta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}P_{K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a)=t^{\delta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}R_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}(q,t,a),

(1.3)

where
\delta(0^{i}10^{j},0^{k}10^{l})=\frac{1}{2}((i+j)(k+l)-j-l+c-1)
, where
c
is the number of components of
K_{0^{i}10^{j},0^{k}10^{l}}
(see §
2.6
). Note that
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
may be a link of more than one component. For the case of
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
a knot, we prove in §
5
the following (see Lemma
5.18
and Theorem
5.19
):

Theorem C
.

For any triangular partition
\tau
, the knot
K_{\tau}
is isotopic to a knot of the form
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
. Conversely, if
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
is a knot (as opposed to a link with more than one component), then
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}=K_{\tau}
for some triangular partition, and moreover
\delta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=|\tau|
.

Furthermore, if
(m,n,\ell)
is a triple of integers such that
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
, then the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
K_{\tau}
is free over
\mathbb{Z}
with Poincaré series given by the triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial up to normalization. Specifically:

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)=(1-q)(at^{-1/2}q^{-1/2})^{-|\tau|}P_{K_{\tau}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a).

In the above theorem (and throughout the rest of the paper), we write
P_{K}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a)
for the Poincaré series of the triply graded Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
K
, and for brevity refer to this as the
KR series
of
K
.

The mapping from triangular partitions to pairs of binary sequences is many-valued, and requires us to choose an expression of
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
, similar to the Schröder polynomials themselves (see §
5.4
). Nonetheless, the knot
K_{\tau}
really only depends on
\tau
up to isotopy, and well-definedness of Khovanov–Rozansky homology establishes the following.

Corollary 1.1
.

The triangular Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
depends only on the triangular partition
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
.

Below we will state some of the most important special cases of Theorem
C
. For combinatorial purposes, it is best to state the result directly in terms of the series
R_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
, using (
1.3
).

(1)

The torus knot
T(m,n)
is
K_{\tau}
where
\tau=\tau_{m,n}
(with
\ell
taken to be the maximum value available,
\ell=mn-1
). The associated pair of binary sequences is
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=(0^{m-1}1,0^{n-1}1)
, and we recover the known identity

S_{m,n}(q,t,a)=t^{\delta(m,n)}R_{0^{m-1}1,0^{n-1}1}(q,t,a),

where
S_{m,n}(q,t,a)=S_{m,n,mn-1}(q,t,a)
is the usual rational Schröder polynomial and
\delta(m,n)
is as in Definition
2.14
.

(2)

The cabled torus knot
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
is
K_{\tau}
, where
\tau=\tau_{md,nd}
(again,
\ell
is taken to be its maximum value
\ell=(md)(nd)-1
). The associated pair of binary sequences is
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=(0^{d-1}10^{(m-1)d},0^{nd}1)

Given integers
m,n,d\geq 1
with
m,n
coprime, the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of
T(m,n)
is the closure of the braid
\beta(,0^{nd}1)\in\mathrm{Br}_{md}
, thus

S_{md,nd}(q,t,a)=t^{\delta(md,nd)}R_{0^{d-1}10^{(m-1)d},0^{nd}1}(q,t,a).

1.3.
The Oblomkov-Rasmussen-Shende Conjecture

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
be a complex plane curve with a singularity at the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and let
K
denote the
link of the singularity

0\in Z
(i.e.
K
is the intersection of
Z
with the 3-sphere of radius
\epsilon
around 0, for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
. A link is
algebraic
if it arises in this way. Algebraic links have been widely studied for past century
[
7
,
40
,
14
]
, with their associated plane curve singularities particularly receiving a lot of attention in recent years
[
54
,
50
,
46
,
52
,
29
,
25
,
53
]
.

The
Oblomkov-Rasmussen-Shende conjecture
asserts that the lowest
a
-degree part of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of an algebraic link
K
is isomorphic to the cohomology of the Hilbert scheme of points on the plane curve singularity whose link is
K
(with the higher
a
-degree components obtained by considering nested Hilbert schemes). We may refer to this conjecture as the ORS1 conjecture, referring to
[
52
]
for the precise statement.

When
K
is a knot, the associated plane curve singularity is called a
unibranch singularity
; in this case ORS propose a second conjecture, which we may refer to as ORS2, which asserts that the lowest
a
-degree part of Khovanov–Rozansky homology of
K
is isomorphic to the cohomology of the
compactified Jacobian
of the singularity
0\in Z
, with its perverse filtration (the higher
a
-degree components are excluded from this conjecture; they were incorporated in a recent preprint by Oscar Kivinen and Minh-Tâm Quang Trinh
[
51
]
). Work of Migliorini and Shende
[
50
]
, and independently Maulik and Yun
[
46
]
, establishes the equivalence of ORS1 and ORS2 (for knots, and only the lowest
a
-degree part).

Ignoring the perverse filtration corresponds to setting
q=1
on the level of Poincaré series. Specialized at
q=1
, the ORS2 conjecture can be stated at the level of Poincaré series as

\Big((1-q)\mathcal{P}^{\text{KR}}_{K}(q,t,a)\Big)|_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
t=u^{-2}\end{subarray}}=a^{\delta}u^{-\delta}\mathcal{P}_{\overline{JC}}(u)+(\text{terms with $a$-degree $>\delta$}),

where
\delta
is the Milnor number of the plane curve singularity whose link is
K
, and
\overline{JC}
is its compactified Jacobian.

In a recent article
[
25
]
, the fourth named author jointly with Gorsky and Oblomkov proved that the compactified Jacobian of (the singularity at the origin of) the plane curve
(x(t),y(t))=(t^{nd},t^{md}+\lambda t^{md+1}+\dots)
has an affine paving with cells indexed by
\tau_{md,nd}
-Dyck paths, with Poincaré series computed by a specialization of the triangular
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomial
C_{\tau_{md,nd}}(q,t)
. Combined with the realization that the link of the singularity in this case is
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
, we obtain a proof of the ORS2 conjecture at
q=1
for these knots.

Theorem D
.

The second Oblomkov-Rasmussen-Shende conjecture at
q=1
holds for the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of the
(m,n)
-torus knot; that is, for
\tau=\tau_{md,nd}
we have:

\displaystyle\Big((1-q)\mathcal{P}^{\text{KR}}_{K_{\tau}}(q,t,a)\Big)|_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
t=u^{-2}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=a^{\delta}u^{-\delta}u^{2\delta}S_{\tau}(q,t,0)+(\text{terms with $a$-degree $>\delta$})

\displaystyle=a^{\delta}u^{-\delta}\mathcal{P}_{\overline{JC}}(u)+(\text{terms with $a$-degree $>\delta$}).

This theorem is restated and proven as Theorem
5.23
in §
5.5
.

1.4.
The
(q,t)
-Schröder Theorem

The classical
shuffle theorem
, conjectured by Haglund-Haiman-Loehr-Remmel-Ulyanov
[
34
]
and proven by Carlsson-Mellit
[
9
]
, gives a combinatorial formula in terms of
\tau_{n,n+1}
-Dyck paths for
\nabla e_{n}
. The
(q,t)
-Schröder Theorem
was conjectured by Egge, Haglund, Killpatrick and Kremer
[
13
]
and proven by Haglund
[
31
]
over ten years before Carlsson and Mellit’s proof of the shuffle theorem. It states that the hook components of the Schur expansion of
\nabla e_{n}
coincide with the classical Schröder polynomials
S_{n,n+1}(q,t,a)
, i.e. the following equality holds:

S_{n,n+1}(q,t,a)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\langle\nabla e_{n},h_{k}e_{n-k}\rangle\;a^{k}.

Motivated by their study of certain superpolynomials in the double affine Hecke algebra and their connection to Khovanov–Rozansky homology of torus knots, Gorsky and Negut
[
28
]
conjectured a rational generalization of the shuffle theorem (which Mellit
[
48
]
proved), wherein
\nabla e_{n}
is replaced by the action of certain operator
P_{m,n}
in the
elliptic Hall algebra
on the element
1
in the ring of symmetric functions and the combinatorial side now ranges over
\tau_{m,n}
-Dyck paths with
m,n
relatively prime integers. The action of the elliptic Hall algebra
\mathcal{E}
on symmetric functions and its connection to Macdonald and
(q,t)
-Catalan combinatorics is well documented with both algebraic and geometric formulations studied by Feigin-Tsymbaliuk
[
17
]
and Burban, Schiffman, and Vasserot
[
8
,
55
,
56
,
57
,
58
]
. Inspired by this construction, Blasiak-Haiman-Morse-Pun-Seelinger
[
6
]
proved that a similar equality could be given by considering certain operators
\mathsf{D}_{\tau_{r,s}}\in\mathcal{E}
indexed by triangular partitions
\tau_{r,s}
, with
r,s
arbitrary positive real numbers:

\mathsf{D}_{\tau_{r,s}}(1)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\omega(\mathcal{G}_{\nu(\pi)}(X;t^{-1})),

where
\omega
is the standard Weyl involution on symmetric functions and
\mathcal{G}_{\nu(\pi)}(X;t^{-1})
is the LLT-polynomial for some particular tuple of skew shapes
\nu(\pi)
. Although this so-called
shuffle theorem under any line
fully generalized the combinatorial side of the previous shuffle theorems, its connections to Khovanov–Rozansky homology and torus links remained completely open. In this article, we make considerable progress towards bridging this gap by showing that the hook components of the Schur expansion of
\mathsf{D}_{\tau}(1)
are exactly given by our triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial
S_{\tau}(q,t,a)
and thus the KR series of the Coxeter knot
K_{\tau}
. Using a superization procedure for LLT-polynomials, we prove:

Theorem E
.

The triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder theorem holds:

S_{\tau_{r,s}}(q,t,a)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t),h_{k}e_{\lfloor s\rfloor-k}\rangle\;a^{k}.

This result is stated and proven as Corollary
6.12
. Note that
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(X;q,t)
is the symmetric function obtained by the action of particular elliptic Hall algebra elements indexed by a triangular partition
\tau
on
1
.

1.5.
Further Directions

There are many open questions that remain to be studied. Although we were able to compute the KR series for all Coxeter
knots
, ideally one would like such a result for all Coxeter
links
and general monotone curves. It is our hope that an extension of our work might yield light in this direction.

Combinatorially there are also several directions worth pursuing. In a recent article, the second named author alongside Simental and Vazirani
[
22
]
gave a ‘finite’ analog of the rational shuffle theorem in which the elliptic Hall algebra is replaced the double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA). Conjecturally, their construction coincides with the bigraded
GL_{r}
-character of certain quantized Gieseker variety representations. On the other hand, Galashin and Lam’s superpolynomial for monotone links is built from the DAHA. It it unknown for exactly which monotone links the superpolynomial agrees with the KR series. Thus, one can ask whether a generalization to the triangular setting of the finite shuffle theorem of González-Simental-Vazirani could yield light on a geometric interpretation of our triangular Schröder polynomials, which combined with the results in this paper might provide a partial answer to when the KR series and the superpolynomial coincide.

1.6.
Structure of the paper:

In §
2
, we recall the necessary topological notions of knots and cables, the construction of Khovanov–Rozansky homology and binary recursion of Hogancamp and Mellit. §
3
is similarly laying the combinatorial background in the form of invariant subsets, the bijection onto Dyck paths, and the Gorsky-Mazin-Vazirani recursions that form the building blocks of the upcoming definitions. §
4
is the combinatorial heart of the article and where Theorems
A
and
B
are proved. Here, triangular partitions are further explored and the
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomials are finally defined. It is in §
5
that most of the main results come together. We recall the work of Galashin-Lam relating monotone and Coxeter knots, and follow up by connecting them further to the binary knots of Hogancamp-Mellit, ending with the proofs of Theorems
C
and
D
. Finally, in §
6
we relate the triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial with the existing Catalan combinatorics. We review the shuffle theorems, LLT polynomials, and superization, before proving Theorem
E
. Lastly, in Appendix
A
we provide an alternative self contained derivation of the standardization procedure of §
6
and in Appendix
B
we give a way of extending the binary sequences in §
5
to certain links.

2.
Khovanov–Rozansky Homology and the Hogancamp-Mellit Recursion

We begin by recalling some basic topological notions.

2.1.
Framed Knots and Cables

Let
D^{2}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\>|\>|z|\leq 1\}
denote the unit disk in the plane
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let
S^{1}=\partial D^{2}
be the unit circle. Let
\mathbb{T}=S^{1}\times S^{1}
denote the 2-dimensional torus. We identify
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
via the mapping
\mathbb{R}\twoheadrightarrow S^{1}
sending
t\mapsto e^{2\pi it}
, which induces an identification of
\mathbb{T}
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(restricting to the unit square
[0,1]^{2}
gives the familiar realization of
\mathbb{T}
as the square with opposite edges identified). The
longitude
of
\mathbb{T}
is the copy of
S^{1}
identified as the image of the
x
-axis
\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
with
leftward orientation
. The
meridian
of
\mathbb{T}
is the copy of
S^{1}
identified as the image of the
y
-axis
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
with its
upward
orientation.

We may embed
\mathbb{T}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in a standard fashion with image the set of points of distance
1/2
from
\{x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}
, so that the meridian of
\mathbb{T}
maps to the circle with parametrization
\theta\mapsto(0,1,0)+\frac{1}{2}(0,-\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))
, and the longitude of
\mathbb{T}
maps to the circle parametrized by
\theta\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta),0)
.

Given relatively prime integers
m,n
, we consider the line segment in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
from
(m,0)
to
(0,n)
. The image of this segment in
\mathbb{T}
is a closed curve, denoted
T(m,n)^{\mathbb{T}}
. The image of
T(m,n)^{\mathbb{T}}
under the standard embedding
\mathbb{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the
(m,n)
-torus knot
, denoted
T(m,n)
. Given
d\geq 1
, let
T(md,nd)^{\mathbb{T}}
denote
d
parallel copies of
T(m,n)^{\mathbb{T}}
in
\mathbb{T}
, whose image in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the torus link
T(md,nd)
.

We will be interested in knots which are obtained as cables of torus knots. In order to discuss these, we must introduce the notion of framed knots.
A
framed knot

\mathcal{K}
is an embedding of the solid torus
S^{1}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, regarded up to isotopy. Given any point
p\in D^{2}
we obtain an ordinary knot
K
by restricting
\mathcal{K}
to an embedding
S^{1}\times\{p\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The knot
K
does not depend on
p
, up to isotopy, and we say that
\mathcal{K}
is a framing of
K
.

If
\mathcal{K}
is a framed knot and
p_{1}\neq p_{2}\in D^{2}
are distinct points, then we may consider knots
K_{i}
obtained as the images of
S^{1}\times\{p_{i}\}
under
\mathcal{K}
. Let
r
denote the
linking number
of the 2-component link
K_{1}\cup K_{2}
(upon choosing a diagram for
K_{1}\cup K_{2}
, the linking number is one half the number of crossings between
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
, counted with signs). This linking number does not depend on the choice of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, and is called the
framing coefficient of
\mathcal{K}
. Equivalently (but perhaps less precise), the framing coefficient of
\mathcal{K}
is the number of twists in the embedded annulus obtained by taking the image of
S^{1}\times a
, where
a\subset D^{2}
is an embedded arc connecting the points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.

Up to isotopy, a framed knot is completely determined by its framing coefficient
r
, and we will abuse notation and write
\mathcal{K}=(K,r)
. The
Seifert framing
of
K
is the unique framing with coefficient
r=0
.

Given a framed knot
\mathcal{K}=(K,r)
, restricting from the solid torus to its boundary yields embedding
\phi_{K,r}\colon\mathbb{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
. When
K=U
is the unknot with Seifert framing, we obtain the standard embedding
\phi_{U,0}\colon\mathbb{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For more general
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, the embedding
\phi_{U,r}
sends the closed curve
T(p,q)^{\mathbb{T}}
to
T(p,q+rp)
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
K
be a knot. The
(p,q)
-cable of
K
, denoted
K(p,q)
, is defined to be the image of
T(p,q)^{\mathbb{T}}
under the embedding
\phi_{K,0}\colon\mathbb{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
introduced above.

Remark 2.2
.

We can also realize
K(p,q)
as the image of
T(p,q-rp)
under
\phi_{K,r}
for any
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, since the effect of changing the framing from 0 to
r
is cancelled by the insertion of
r
negative full twists (in the meridian direction) into
T(p,q)^{\mathbb{T}}
, obtaining
T(p,q-rp)^{\mathbb{T}}
.

2.2.
Braid Presentations of Some Special Knots

In this paper we are particularly interested in the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of the torus knot
T(m,n)
, denoted
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
, where
m,n,d
are positive integers with
m,n
relatively prime.
These knots are the simplest instances of algebraic knots (see §
5.5
). Note that this family includes positive torus knots as the special case
d=1
. In this section we will focus on finding a braid presentation of these knots.

Let
\mathrm{Br}_{n}
denote the
braid group
on
n
strands. Let
\mathbbm{1}_{n}\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}
denote the identity braid. For
1\leq i\leq n-1
let
\sigma_{i}
denote the standard Artin generator, and let
\mathrm{cox}_{n}:=\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{n-1}\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}
denote the
standard Coxeter braid
on
n
strands. These are pictured in Figure
1
.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Figure 1.
The standard Coxeter generators of the braid group (left) and the standard Coxeter braid (right).

Given braids
\beta_{1}\in\mathrm{Br}_{n_{1}}
and
\beta_{2}\in\mathrm{Br}_{n_{2}}
, let
\beta_{1}\sqcup\beta_{2}\in\mathrm{Br}_{n_{1}+n_{2}}
denote the union of the braids
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
. In terms of the diagrams above,
\beta_{1}\sqcup\beta_{2}
consists of
\beta_{2}
above
\beta_{1}
. Given a braid
\beta
, let
\hat{\beta}
denote the oriented link obtained by closing
\beta
.

Example 2.3
.

For integers
M,N
with
M\geq 1
, the torus link
T(M,N)
is the closure of the braid
\mathrm{cox}_{M}^{N}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

For
m,n
coprime and
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we have
T(md,nd)=T(m,n)(d,mnd)
.

Proof.

By definition,
T(md,nd)
consists of
d
parallel copies of
T(m,n)
, where the notion of “parallel” comes from the embedding in the standard torus
\mathbb{T}
. On the other hand,
T(m,n)(d,mnd)
is the image of
T(d,0)^{\mathbb{T}}
under the embedding
\phi_{T(m,n),mn}
; this consists of
d
parallel copies of
T(m,n)
, where the notion of “parallel” comes from the framing coefficient
mn
, so that two distinct copies of
T(m,n)
in
\phi_{T(m,n),mn}(T(d,0)^{\mathbb{T}})
have linking number
mn
. To prove the lemma, we just need to see that any two distinct copies of
T(m,n)
in
T(md,nd)
have linking number
mn
. To see this, assume without loss of generality that
d=2
. Then we have a presentation of
T(2m,2n)
as the closure of the braid
(\mathrm{cox}_{2m})^{2n}
. Of the
2n(2m-1)
crossings,
2mn
of these involve the two distinct components, so the linking number between these components is
mn
, which proves the lemma.
∎

Figure 2.
Realizing
T(6,8)
as the
(2,24)
cable of
T(3,4)
.

From Lemma
2.4
we deduce the following.

Lemma 2.5
.

T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
can be represented as the closure of
(\mathrm{cox}_{d}\sqcup\mathbbm{1}_{(m-1)d})(\mathrm{cox}_{md})^{nd}
.

Proof.

Lemma
2.4
tells us that
T(m,n)(d,mnd)=T(md,nd)
. It is known that
T(md,nd)
can be represented as the closure of the braid
(\mathrm{cox}_{md})^{nd}
. To obtain
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
from
T(m,n)(d,mnd)
, insert one more copy of the braid
\mathrm{cox}_{d}
, and the statement follows.
∎

2.3.
Soergel Bimodules and Rouquier Complexes

We briefly recall the main ideas needed for the construction of Khovanov–Rozansky homology and refer the reader to
[
42
,
41
,
43
,
15
,
39
,
16
,
49
]
for detailed exposure on this topic.

Fix an integer
n\geq 0
and let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative ring. Let
R=\mathbb{K}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
regarded as a graded ring with
\deg(x_{i})=2
. If
B
is a graded
(R,R)
-bimodule and
k\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, then we denote by
q^{k}B
the bimodule
B
with grading “shifted up by
2k
units”; that is,
(q^{k}B)_{i}=B_{i-2k}
. Let
\star=\otimes_{R}
denote the monoidal operation on bimodules, and let
\boxtimes=\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}
denote the external tensor product.

If
X
is a complex of graded
(R,R)
-bimodules, then we let
\Omega X
denote the cohomological shift; that is, the
i
-th chain group of
\Omega X
is
X^{i-1}
, with negated differential
d_{\Omega X}=-d_{X}
.

Given
k\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
, we let
t^{k}X:=\Omega^{2k}q^{-k}X
. Observe that
q,t,\Omega
are not independent, but satisfy the relation

q^{\frac{1}{2}}t^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Omega.

Figure 3.
A braid whose closure is
T(3,4)(3,37)
, shown here as embedded in a pair-of-pants surface.

We let
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n}
denote the category of
Soergel bimodules
for the symmetric group
S_{n}

[
16
]
. This is a monoidal subcategory of the category of graded
(R,R)
-bimodules. We denote by
\operatorname{Ch}^{b}(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n})
the category of bounded chain complexes in
\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n}
.

The grading shifts interact with
\operatorname{Hom}
spaces as follows:

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n}}(B,q^{k}B^{\prime})=q^{k}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n}}(B,B^{\prime})\ ,\qquad\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n}}(q^{l}B,B^{\prime})=q^{-l}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n}}(B,B^{\prime}).

Now, for
1\leq i\leq n-1
, we let

B_{i}:=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}R\otimes_{R^{s_{i}}}R,

where
s_{i}
is the simple transposition
(i,i+1)
in the symmetric group
S_{n}
, and
R^{s_{i}}\subset R
is the graded subalgebra of
s_{i}
-invariant polynomials. We have canonical degree zero maps

B_{i}\rightarrow q^{-\frac{1}{2}}R\ \text{given by}\ f\otimes g\mapsto fg

and

q^{\frac{1}{2}}R\rightarrow B_{i}\ \text{given by}\ 1\mapsto x_{i}\otimes 1-1\otimes x_{i+1}.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
T_{i}^{\pm 1}=T(\sigma_{i}^{\pm 1})
be following two-term complexes:

T_{i}:=\left(t^{-1/2}B_{i}\rightarrow R\right)\quad\text{and}\qquad T_{i}^{-1}:=\left(R\rightarrow t^{1/2}B_{i}\right).

The
Rouquier complex
associated to a braid
\beta=\sigma^{\pm}_{i_{1}}\dots\sigma^{\pm}_{i_{k}}\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}
is the chain complex
T(\beta)\in\operatorname{Ch}^{b}(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n})
obtained by tensoring

T(\sigma^{\pm}_{i_{1}}),\dots,T(\sigma^{\pm}_{i_{k}}).

Remark 2.7
.

The above conventions for
q
and
t
will enable a direct comparison between our computations of
\operatorname{HHH}
and the
(q,t)
-Catalan numbers. It is more traditional in Soergel bimodule literature to denote the grading and homological shifts of
X
by
X(1)
and
X[1]
, respectively; these are related to our
q,t,\Omega
by

X(1)=q^{-\frac{1}{2}}X\ ,\qquad X[1]=\Omega^{-1}X.

2.4.
Khovanov–Rozansky Homology

Let
\mathbbm{k}\text{-Mod}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}
denote the category of bigraded
\mathbb{K}
-modules. Let
\operatorname{HH}^{\cdot}\colon\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n}\to\mathbbm{k}\text{-Mod}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}
be the
\mathbb{K}
-linear functor sending a graded
(R,R)
-bimodule to its Hochschild cohomology. Extending to complexes gives us a functor

\operatorname{HH}^{\cdot}\colon\operatorname{Ch}^{b}(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n})\to\operatorname{Ch}^{b}(\mathbbm{k}\text{-Mod}^{\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}).

The complex
\operatorname{HH}^{\cdot}(X)
is triply graded via

\operatorname{HH}^{i,j,k}(X):=\text{Soergel degree-$i$ component of }\operatorname{HH}^{j}(X^{k}).

The homology of
\operatorname{HH}^{\cdot}(X)
is denoted by

\operatorname{HHH}^{\cdot}(X):=H^{\cdot}(\operatorname{HH}^{\cdot}(X)).

On triply graded
\mathbb{K}
-modules, we will use the grading shifts
q,a,t
defined by

(qM)^{i,j,k}=M^{i-2,j,k}\ ,\qquad(aM)^{i,j,k}=M^{i+2,j-1,k}\ ,\qquad(tM)^{i,j,k}=M^{i+2,j,k-2},

and the cohomological shift

(\Omega M)^{i,j,k}=M^{i,j,k-1}.

Note that
qt=\Omega^{2}
. Note also that
a
acts by shifing up by 1 in the Hochschild degree, together with a shift down by 2 in the Soergel bimodule degree.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\beta\in\mathrm{Br}_{n}
and
e=e(\beta)
be the number of positive crossings minus the number of negative crossings, and let
c=c(\beta)
denote the number of components of the link represented by
\beta
. Define the
normalizing exponent
of the braid
\beta
by the formula

\delta(\beta):=\frac{1}{2}(e(\beta)+c(\beta)-n(\beta)).

(2.1)

The
Khovanov–Rozansky complex
of
\beta
is

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{KR}}(\beta)

\displaystyle:=(at^{1/2}q^{-1/2})^{\delta(\beta)}\operatorname{HH}^{\cdot}(T(\beta)).

Its homology is the
Khovanov–Rozansky
homology of the link
\widehat{\beta}
represented by
\beta
, and is denoted by
H_{\mathrm{KR}}(\beta):=(at^{1/2}q^{-1/2})^{\delta(\beta)}\operatorname{HHH}(T(\beta))
.

Remark 2.9
.

If
K
is a positive knot and
\beta
is a positive braid representing
K
, then the normalizing exponent
\delta(\beta)
agrees with the
genus
of
K
(in general,
\delta(\beta)
is the genus of the Seifert surface produced by Seifert’s algorithm). In particular, for positive braids
\delta(\beta)
depends only on the braid closure, hence is actually a well-defined knot invariant. Note that all the braids of interest in this paper are positive, so this remark applies.

Recall that for any tri-graded
\mathbb{K}
-module
M=\bigoplus_{i,j,k}M^{i,j,k}
, the
Poincaré series
of
M
computes the sum of the graded ranks over
\mathbb{K}
; namely,

\mathcal{P}(M)=\sum_{i,j,k}q^{i}t^{j}a^{k}\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}}(M^{i,j,k}).

Definition 2.10
.

The
Khovanov–Rozansky (KR) series
of a link
L=\widehat{\beta}
is defined to be
\mathcal{P}_{L}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a):=\mathcal{P}(H_{\mathrm{KR}}(\beta))
.

Theorem 2.11

(
[
42
,
41
]
)
.

If
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
represent isotopic links, then there is a homotopy equivalence
C_{\mathrm{KR}}(\beta)\simeq C_{\mathrm{KR}}(\beta^{\prime})
, and consequently
\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{\beta}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a)=\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{\beta^{\prime}}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a)
.

Example 2.12
.

For non-negative integers
M,N\geq 0
, the torus link
T(M,N)
is the closure of
\mathrm{cox}_{M}^{N}
. For this braid we have
e=(M-1)N
and
c=\gcd(M,N)
, hence

\delta=\frac{MN-M-N+\gcd(M,N)}{2}.

Example 2.13
.

For integers
m,n,d\geq 0
with
m,n
coprime, we consider the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of
T(m,n)
as the closure of
(\mathrm{cox}_{d}\sqcup\mathbbm{1}_{(m-1)d})\mathrm{cox}_{md}^{nd}
, as in Lemma
2.5
. For this braid we have
e=(d-1)+(md-1)(nd)
, with
c=1
and number of strands
md
, hence

\delta=\frac{(md)(nd)-md-nd+d}{2}.

The integers
\delta
appearing in the above examples will occur frequently in this paper, and we will abbreviate them.

Definition 2.14
.

For integers
M,N\geq 0
, we let
\delta(M,N):=\frac{MN-M-N+\gcd(M,N)}{2}
.

2.5.
Links from Binary Sequences

Definition 2.15
.

If
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})\in\{0,1\}^{m}
, we let
|\mathbf{u}|=\sum_{i}u_{i}
.
Given sequences
\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=|\mathbf{v}|
, we define a complex
C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\in\operatorname{Ch}^{b}(\mathbb{S}\mathrm{Bim}_{n})
as follows. Abbreviate by writing
l=|\mathbf{u}|=|\mathbf{v}|
. Let
\pi_{\mathbf{u}}\in S_{m}
denote the shuffle permutation corresponding to
\mathbf{u}
, i.e. the permutation given in one-line notation by
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},j_{1},\ldots,j_{l})
with
k+l=m
, where
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
are the indices for which
u_{i}=0
, and
j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}
are the indices for which
u_{j}=1
. Let
\beta_{\mathbf{u}}
denote the positive braid lift of
\pi_{\mathbf{u}}^{-1}
. Next, write
\mathbf{v}
as
\mathbf{v}=(0^{r_{0}}10^{r_{1}}1\cdots 10^{r_{l}})
, and set

\gamma_{m,\mathbf{v}}:=(\mathrm{cox}_{m})^{r_{0}}(\mathbbm{1}_{1}\boxtimes\mathrm{cox}_{m-1})^{r_{1}}\cdots(\mathbbm{1}_{l}\boxtimes\mathrm{cox}_{m-l})^{r_{l}}.

Finally, let
\beta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\beta_{\mathbf{u}}\gamma_{m,\mathbf{v}}
, and

C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}):=T(\beta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}))\star(K_{l}\boxtimes\mathbbm{1}_{n-l}),

where
K_{l}
is the complex constructed in
[
15
,
39
]
. When
l=1
we let
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
denote the closure of the braid
\beta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
.

Example 2.16
.

If
\mathbf{u}=(10001)
and
\mathbf{v}=(011000)
, then
C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
may be pictured as in Figure
4
.

Figure 4.
The complex
C({\bf u,v})
for
\mathbf{u}=(10001)
and
\mathbf{v}=(011000)
.

The following is proven in
[
39
]
.

Lemma 2.17
.

If
|\mathbf{u}|=|\mathbf{v}|=1
, then
\operatorname{HHH}(\beta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}))\cong\operatorname{HHH}(C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}))\otimes\mathbb{K}[x_{1}]
.

Lemma 2.18
.

For
\mathbf{u}=0^{i}10^{j}
and
\mathbf{v}=0^{k}10^{l}
we have that
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
is the closure of the braid

\beta(0^{i}10^{j},0^{k}10^{l})=(\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{i})(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{m-1})^{k}(\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{m-1})^{l}

with normalizing exponent (
2.1
) given by

\delta(\beta(0^{i}10^{j},0^{k}10^{l}))=\frac{(i+j)(k+l)-j-l+c-1}{2}.

(2.2)

Proof.

Immediate from the definitions.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

Given integers
m,n,d\geq 0
with
m,n
coprime, the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of
T(m,n)
coincides with
K_{0^{d-1}10^{(m-1)d},0^{nd}1}
. Moreover, the normalizing exponent (
2.2
) agrees with
\delta(md,nd)
from Definition
2.14
.

Proof.

From the definitions we have
\beta(0^{d-1}10^{(m-1)d},0^{nd}1)=(\mathrm{cox}_{d}\sqcup\mathbbm{1}_{d(m-1)})\mathrm{cox}_{md}^{nd}
, whose closure is the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of the torus knot
T(m,n)
, by Lemma
2.5
. Comparison of the normalizing exponent with
\delta(md,nd)
is elementary.
∎

Construction 2.20
.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{M}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
be binary sequences with
|\mathbf{u}|=|\mathbf{v}|=1
. We provide a description of the link
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
from Definition
2.15
. First, choose real numbers
0<y_{1}<\cdots<y_{M}<1
and
0<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{N}<1
. Consider the unit square
[0,1]^{2}
with the following set of points on its boundary:

S=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq M}(0,y_{i})\cup\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq N}(x_{j},0)\cup\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq M\\
i\neq a\end{subarray}}(1,y_{i})\cup\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq N\\
j\neq b\end{subarray}}(x_{j},1),

where
a
is the index with
u_{a}=1
and
b
is the index with
v_{b}=1
.
Let
D
be union of arcs of the following types:

(1)

Consider the ordered tuples of boundary points

\displaystyle(p_{1},\ldots,p_{M+N-1})

\displaystyle=\Big((0,y_{1}),\ldots,(0,y_{M}),(x_{1},1),\ldots,\widehat{(x_{b},1)},\ldots,(x_{N},1)\Big),

\displaystyle(q_{1},\ldots,p_{M+N-1})

\displaystyle=\Big((x_{1},0),\ldots,(x_{N},0),(1,y_{1}),\ldots\widehat{(1,y_{a})},\ldots,(1,y_{M})\Big).

For
1\leq k\leq M+N-1
, let
A_{k}
denote the straight line segment from
p_{k}
to
q_{k}
.

(2)

For each
1\leq i\leq M
with
i\neq a
, let
H_{i}
denote an arc connecting
(0,y_{i})
to
(1,y_{i})
, disjoint from the interior of
[0,1]^{2}
. Assume that the
H_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq M
, are pairwise disjoint.

(3)

For each
1\leq j\leq N
with
j\neq b
, let
V_{j}
denote an arc connecting
(x_{j},0)
to
(x_{j},1)
, beneath the arcs
A_{k}
, and disjoint from each
H_{i}
. Assume that the
V_{j}
, for
1\leq j\leq N
, are pairwise disjoint.

(4)

The only boundary points that remain unpaired are
(0,y_{a})
and
(x_{b},0)
. Choose
\epsilon>0
and let
B_{-}
be an arc connecting
(0,y_{a})
to
(-\epsilon,-\epsilon)
below the all arcs
H_{i}
(and disjoint from all other arcs); let
B_{+}
be an arc connecting
(-\epsilon,-\epsilon)
to
(x_{b},0)
above all arcs
V_{j}
(and disjoint from all other arcs). Let
B=B_{-}\cup B_{+}
.

The union of all arcs of the form
A_{k},H_{i},V_{j}
and
B
is a closed, piecewise linear diagram which represents
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
.

Example 2.21
.

Construction
2.20
is illustrated for
\mathbf{u}=0010
and
\mathbf{v}=01000
in Figure
5
. The arc
B
has been drawn in orange in order to facilitate the comparison with the monotone link.

2.20
illustrated for
\mathbf{u}=0010
and

.

2.6.
The Hogancamp-Mellit Recursion

In
[
39
]
, the third named author and Mellit presented a recursive computation of
\operatorname{HHH}(C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}))
(for

(C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
as defined in Definition
2.15
), which we now recall.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
{\bf u}\in\{0,1\}^{m+\ell}
and
{\bf v}\in\{0,1\}^{n+\ell}
be two binary sequences with
|{\bf u}|=|{\bf v}|=\ell
. Define
R_{{\bf u},{\bf v}}\in\mathbb{N}[q,t^{\pm},a,(1-q)^{-1}]
as the power series uniquely determined by the following recursions:

\displaystyle R_{0{\bf u},0{\bf v}}

\displaystyle=t^{-|{\bf u}|}R_{{\bf u}1,{\bf v}1}+qt^{-|{\bf u}|}R_{{\bf u}0,{\bf v}0},

\displaystyle R_{1{\bf u},0{\bf v}}

\displaystyle=R_{{\bf u}1,{\bf v}},

\displaystyle R_{\emptyset,0^{n}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1+a}{1-q}\right)^{n},

\displaystyle R_{1{\bf u},1{\bf v}}

\displaystyle=(t^{|{\bf u}|}+a)R_{{\bf u},{\bf v}},

\displaystyle R_{0{\bf u},1{\bf v}}

\displaystyle=R_{{\bf u},{\bf v}1},

\displaystyle R_{0^{m},\emptyset}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1+a}{1-q}\right)^{m},

where
R_{\emptyset,\emptyset}:=1
.

Theorem 2.23

(
[
39
]
)
.

For any two binary sequences
{\bf u}
and
{\bf v}
with
|{\bf u}|=|{\bf v}|
,
we have

\mathcal{P}(\operatorname{HHH}^{\cdot}(C(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})))=R_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}.

In particular, if
|\mathbf{u}|=|\mathbf{v}|=1
, then the KR series of the link
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}:=\hat{\beta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
is given by

\mathcal{P}_{K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a)=\frac{(at^{1/2}q^{-1/2})^{\delta(\beta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}))}}{1-q}R_{\bf u,v}(q,t,a),

(2.3)

where
\delta(\beta)
is the normalizing exponent (
2.1
).

Remark 2.24
.

The denominator
\frac{1}{1-q}
in (
2.3
) comes from the tensor factor
\mathbb{K}[x_{1}]
in Lemma
2.17
.

Remark 2.25
.

The special case
{\bf u}=0^{M-1}1
,
{\bf v}=0^{N-1}1
yields the KR series of
T(M,N)
. One of the central aims of this paper is to give a combinatorial interpretation of
K_{\bf u,v}
and its KR series, when
K_{\bf u,v}
is a knot.

This together with Lemma
2.19
gives the following.

Corollary 2.26
.

For integers
m,n,d\geq 0
with
m,n
coprime, the KR series of the cable knot
T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)
is given by

\mathcal{P}_{T(m,n)(d,mnd+1)}=\frac{(at^{1/2}q^{-1/2})^{\delta(md,nd)}}{1-q}R_{(0^{d-1}10^{(m-1)d},0^{nd}1)}.

∎

Here we are using
\delta(md,nd)
from Definition
2.14
, and the computation from Example
2.13
.

3.
Dyck paths, Invariant Subsets, and the Gorsky-Mazin-Vazirani Recursion

In this article a
box
or
cell
will be a unit square of the form
[x-1,x]\times[y-1,y]
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. The point
(x,y)
is the
northeast (NE) corner
of this box, while
(x-1,y-1)
is the
southwest (SW) corner
. We will generally identify a box
\Box
with its NE corner, thereby labeling boxes by elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

Given two real numbers
r,s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},
consider the line
L_{r,s}:=\{x/r+y/s=1\}
connecting points
(r,0)
and
(0,s)
on the coordinate axes.

Definition 3.1
.

Given a line
L_{r,s}
, we say that a box
\Box=(x,y)

lies on
L
, if we have inequalities
(x-1)/r+(y-1)/s\leq 1
(which expresses that the SW corner of
\Box
lies weakly below
L_{r,s}
) and
x/r+y/s>1
(which expresses that the NE corner of
\Box
lies strictly above
L_{r,s}
). We say that
\Box
is below
L
if
x/r+y/s\leq 1
, and above
L_{r,s}
if
(x-1)/r+(y-1)/s>1
.

Given an integer
m
, let
[m]:=\{1,\ldots,m\}
. We will identify a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell})
with its associated Young diagram (written in the French style) so that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{\ell}
.

Definition 3.2

(
[
5
]
)
.

A partition
\tau
is
triangular
if there exists a line
L_{r,s}=\{x/r+y/s=1\}
such that
\tau
consists of all the boxes contained between
L_{r,s}
and the coordinate axes. If so, we write
\tau=\tau_{r,s}
and say that
L_{r,s}

cuts out

\tau_{r,s}.
Note that the same triangular partition can be cut out by different lines (see Figure
6
).

Figure 6.
The partition
(4,2,1)
is triangular and is cut out by different lines; for instance
(4,2,1)=\tau_{3.9,5.9}=\tau_{4.6,5.2}=\tau_{3.6,6.6}.

Definition 3.3
.

A
Dyck path
is a pair
\pi=(\lambda,\tau)
, where
\tau
is a triangular partition and
\lambda\subset\tau
is a subpartition. We refer to
(\lambda,\tau)
as a
\tau
-Dyck path
, and write
\mathrm{D}(\tau)
for the set of all such pairs. When
\tau=\tau_{r,s}
we write
\mathrm{D}(r,s):=\mathrm{D}(\tau_{r,s})
and refer to elements of this set as
r,s
-Dyck paths
.

The
area
of a Dyck path
\pi=(\lambda,\tau)
is defined as

\mathtt{area}(\pi):=|\tau|-|\lambda|.

3.1.
Rational Dyck Paths and Anderson Fillings

For the remainder of this section we mostly specialize to the case when
r,s
are relatively prime integers and will resume the general triangular setting in §
4
. For the most part, the results in this subsection are well known and straightforward but we include the proofs for completeness.

So then, let
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be relatively prime integers and consider the rectangular diagram
[m]\times[n]
. We may decompose
[m]\times[n]
as a disjoint union of three subsets:

[m]\times[n]=\tau_{m,n}\sqcup\mathrm{diag}_{m,n}\sqcup\tau_{m,n}^{\vee},

where
\mathrm{diag}_{m,n}
is the set of boxes that lie on the diagonal line
L_{m,n},

\tau_{m,n}
is the corresponding triangular partition, and
\tau_{m,n}^{\vee}
consists of those boxes in
[m]\times[n]
which lie above the diagonal.

Remark 3.4
.

Since
m,n
are relatively prime, there are no integer solutions to
nx+my=nm
with
(x,y)\in[m]\times[n]
. This implies that the strict inequalities
nx+my<nm
and
nx^{\prime}+my^{\prime}>nm
are equivalent to the corresponding weak inequalities for boxes in
[m]\times[n]
.

Note that the cardinality of
[m]\times[n]
is
mn
, the cardinality of
\mathrm{diag}_{m,n}
is
n+m-1
, and
|\tau_{m,n}|=|\tau_{m,n}^{\vee}|
by symmetry, which forces

|\tau_{m,n}|=\frac{(m-1)(n-1)}{2}.

The following lemma is elementary but quite useful.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
m,n\geq 1
be coprime integers. Suppose we are given integers
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
nx+my=nx^{\prime}+my^{\prime}
. Then
y,y^{\prime}\in[n]
implies
(x,y)=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
(by symmetry
x,x^{\prime}\in[m]
implies
(x,y)=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
as well).

Proof.

Suppose
nx+my=nx^{\prime}+my^{\prime}
for some boxes
(x,y)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
in
Z\times[n]
. This implies that
|y-y^{\prime}|
is divisible by
n
, and
1\leq y,y^{\prime}
forces
|y-y^{\prime}|<n
, which then forces
y-y^{\prime}=0
. This then implies
nx=nx^{\prime}
, hence
x=x^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 3.6

(
[
2
]
)
.

For coprime integers
m,n\geq 1
, the
(m,n)
-Anderson filling
is the function
\gamma_{m,n}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
defined by

\gamma_{m,n}(x,y)=mn-nx-my.

Given a box
\Box
with NE corner
(x,y)
, we refer to
\gamma_{m,n}(\Box):=\gamma_{m,n}(x,y)
as the
(m,n)
-Anderson label of
\Box
. When
m,n
are understood, we will write
\gamma=\gamma_{m,n}
and use the terms Anderson filling and label unambigiously.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\Gamma_{m,n}
denote the additive submonoid of
\mathbb{Z}
generated by
m
and
n
. That is,
\Gamma_{m,n}=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\>|\>k=nx+my\text{ for some $x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$}\}
.
\Gamma_{m,n}
is also called the
semigroup generated by
m
and
n.

The main relevant properties of the Anderson filling are stated in the lemma below. Before we state it, we make some very simple observations. First, the fact that
\gamma(x-m,y+n)=\gamma(x,y)
means that
\gamma
descends to a well-defined function on the quotient
\gamma^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\langle(-m,n)\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}
. Secondly, modulo the equivalence relation
(x,y)\sim(x-m,y+n)
, every cell in
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
is equivalent to a unique cell in
\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
. That is to say, the obvious composition

\mathbb{Z}\times[n]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\twoheadrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z})/\langle(-m,n)\rangle

(3.1)

is a bijection.

Lemma 3.8
.

For coprime integers
m,n\geq 1
, the Anderson filling
\gamma=\gamma_{m,n}
satisfies:

(1)

\gamma(\Box)>0
if and only if the box
\Box
is strictly below the line
nx+my=nm
.

(2)

\gamma
induces a bijection
\gamma^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/\langle(-m,n)\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

\gamma
restricts to a bijection
\mathbb{Z}\times[n]\to\mathbb{Z}
.

(4)

we have
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times[n]
if and only if
\gamma(x,y)\in\Gamma_{m,n}.

Proof.

Statement (1) is clear from the definitions.

For statement (2), observe that
\gamma^{\prime}
is surjective since
\gamma
is. To prove that
\gamma^{\prime}
is injective, note that modulo
(-m,n)
, every
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is equivalent to a cell in
\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
, and Lemma
3.5
tells us that
\gamma
restricted to
\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
is injective. This proves statement (2), while precomposing with the bijection (
3.1
) proves statement (3).

For statement (4), observe that

•

(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(0)=(-1,n)
,

•

(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(k+n)=(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(k)+(-1,0)
,

•

(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(k+m)=(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(k)+(0,-1)
(modulo
(k,0)\sim(k-m,n)
).

These observations make it clear that
(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}(\Gamma_{m,n})
equals
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times[n]
, which completes the proof of statement (4).
∎

Definition 3.9
.

When
(r,s)=(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{>0}
are integers, it is natural to identify an
m,n
-Dyck path
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})
with the lattice path
\tilde{\pi}
from
(m,0)
to
(0,n)
following the boundary of
\lambda
(hence the name ‘Dyck path’). To avoid confusion, we will call
\tilde{\pi}
the
associated lattice path
of
\pi
.

Remark 3.10
.

Note that the associated lattice path depends on the choice of the integers
(m,n).
For instance, one has
\tau_{n,n+1}=\tau_{n+1,n}
and
\mathrm{D}(n,n+1)=\mathrm{D}(n+1,n),
but the associated lattice paths are slightly different.

3.2.
The
\mathtt{dinv}
statistic

The
dinv
statistic was introduced by Mark Haiman in the early
2000
’s
[
35
,
36
]
, but didn’t appear in print until
2009
, at least not in full generality.

Definition 3.11

(
[
45
]
)
.

Given any partition
\lambda
, define
\mathtt{dinv}_{p}(\lambda)
to be the cardinality of the following set:

\mathtt{dinv}_{p}(\lambda):=\Big|\left\{\Box\in\lambda:\frac{\mathrm{leg}(\Box)}{\mathrm{arm}(\Box)+1}<p\leq\frac{\mathrm{leg}(\Box)+1}{\mathrm{arm}(\Box)}\right\}\Big|,

where
\mathrm{leg}(\Box)
and
\mathrm{arm}(\Box)
denote the numbers of boxes in
\lambda
that lie strictly above and in the same column and strictly right and in the same row as
\Box
, respectively (see Figure
7
).

\overbrace{\hskip 113.81102pt}
leg
\underbrace{\hskip 170.71652pt}
arm
c

Figure 7.
The arm and leg of a box
c
in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
denoted in red and blue, respectively. The orange and purple diagonal lines denote the lines with slopes
\frac{\mathrm{leg}(c)}{\mathrm{arm}(c)+1}
and
\frac{\mathrm{leg}(c)+1}{\mathrm{arm}(c)}
used to determine the contribution of
c
towards
\mathtt{dinv}
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
(r,s)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}
be a pair of positive real numbers. Then for an
r,s
-Dyck path
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{r,s})
, set

\mathtt{dinv}_{s/r}(\pi):=\mathtt{dinv}_{s/r}(\lambda).

Note that the statistic
\mathtt{dinv}_{s/r}
depends not only on the partition
\tau_{r,s}
and the set
\mathrm{D}(r,s),
but also on the slope
s/r.
For instance, one has
\tau_{n,n+1}=\tau_{n+1,n}
and
\mathrm{D}(n,n+1)=\mathrm{D}(n+1,n),
but
\mathtt{dinv}_{n/(n+1)}
and
\mathtt{dinv}_{(n+1)/n}
are two different statistics. However, when the slope
s/r
(or
n/m
) is understood from the context, we will skip the subscript and use
\mathtt{dinv}
instead of
\mathtt{dinv}_{s/r}
(or
\mathtt{dinv}_{n/m}
).

3.3.
Invariant Subsets

Let
(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{>0}
be a pair of relatively prime integers. In this case, it will be more convenient for us to use an alternative definition of the
\mathtt{dinv}=\mathtt{dinv}_{n/m}
statistic originally inspired by the geometry of compactified Jacobians and developed in
[
24
]
. See also
[
47
]
and
[
26
]
for a simpler proof of the equivalence of the two definitions of
\mathtt{dinv}.
To do so, we need some additional combinatorial tools. We follow the notations from
[
27
]
.

Remark 3.13
.

Note that in
[
27
]
, the more general case where
m
and
n
are not necessarily relatively prime is considered. However, we only need the relatively prime case in this paper, and therefore we simplify the notations accordingly.

Definition 3.14
.

For each subset
\Delta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
\Delta+n:=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\>|\>k-n\in\Delta\}
and denote by
\overline{\Delta}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\Delta
, the set complement of
\Delta
. We say
\Delta
is an
(m,n)
-invariant set
if
\Delta+n\subset\Delta
,
\Delta+m\subset\Delta.
We will denote the set of all
(m,n)
-invariant subsets by
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}
and call the elements of
\overline{\Delta}
the
gaps
in
\Delta
.
In the case that
0\in\Delta
, we say
\Delta
is
0-normalized
and write
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
for the collection of all such sets.

The sets of
n
-generators,
m
-generators
and
co-generators
of any
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathtt{ngen}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\Delta\setminus(\Delta+n)=\{k\in\Delta\>|\>k-n\not\in\Delta\},

\displaystyle\mathtt{mgen}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\Delta\setminus(\Delta+m)=\{k\in\Delta\>|\>k-m\not\in\Delta\},

\displaystyle\mathtt{cogen}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\Delta\>|\>k+n\in\Delta,k+m\in\Delta\}

\displaystyle\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\mathtt{cogen}(\Delta)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

In particular, there is precisely one generator per equivalence class (mod
n
or
m
), so that
|\mathtt{ngen}|=n
and
|\mathtt{mgen}|=m
.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
[k,k+L]=\{k,k+1,\dots,k+L\}\in\mathbb{Z}
. For any
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}
, set:

\displaystyle\mathtt{area}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=|\overline{\Delta}|,

\displaystyle\mathtt{codinv}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k\in\mathtt{ngen}(\Delta)}|[k,k+m-1]\cap\overline{\Delta}|=\sum_{k\in\mathtt{ngen}(\Delta)}|\{k\leq x<k+m\>|\>x\not\in\Delta\}|,

so that area counts the number of gaps of
\Delta
and codinv the number of gaps in length
m
intervals whose starting value is an
n
-generator. We also define:

\displaystyle\delta(m,n)

\displaystyle:=\frac{(m-1)(n-1)}{2},

\displaystyle\mathtt{coarea}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\delta(m,n)-\mathtt{area}(\Delta),

\displaystyle\mathtt{dinv}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\delta(m,n)-\mathtt{codinv}(\Delta).

Note that the definition of
\delta(m,n)
above agrees with Definition
2.14
. An
\mathtt{area}
and
\mathtt{dinv}
preserving bijection between
\mathrm{D}(m,n)
and
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
was introduced in
[
24
]
:

Definition 3.16

(
[
24
]
)
.

Let
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})
be a
m,n
-Dyck path, then the set of
gaps
of
\pi
is given by

\mathrm{Gaps}(\pi):=\{\gamma_{m,n}(\Box)|\Box\in\tau_{m,n}\setminus\lambda\}.

The bijection
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}:\mathrm{D}(m,n)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
is then defined by

\mathcal{A}_{m,n}(\pi):=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\mathrm{Gaps}(\pi).

Notice that
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}
depends on the choice of the relatively prime integers
(m,n),
not only on the partition
\tau_{m,n}
and the set
\mathrm{D}(m,n).
Nonetheless, whenever
(m,n)
are clear from the context, we will skip the subscript and write
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{m,n}.

Definition 3.17
.

For a partition
\lambda
, a box
\Box\not\in\lambda
is called
addable
if
\lambda\cup\Box
is a partition diagram. Equivalently,
\Box
is addable if both its south and west boundaries intersect the boundary of
\lambda.
Thus, a box is addable for a Dyck path
\pi=(\lambda,\tau)
if it is addable for
\lambda
.

Let
\tilde{\pi}
be the associated lattice path of
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})\in\mathrm{D}(m,n).
Then under this bijection, we have the following correspondences:

\displaystyle\{\gamma(x,y)\;|\;\text{ the top boundary of the box }(x,y)\text{ is a horizontal step of }\tilde{\pi}\}=\mathtt{mgen}(\mathcal{A}(\pi)),

\displaystyle\{\gamma(x,y)\;|\;\text{ the right boundary of the box }(x,y)\text{ is the a vertical step of }\tilde{\pi}\}=\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}(\pi)),

\displaystyle\{\gamma(x,y)\ \;|\;(x,y)\text{ is an addable box of }\lambda\}=\mathtt{cogen}(\mathcal{A}(\pi)).

See Figure
8
for an example.

12
8
4
0
9
5
1
-3
6
2
-2
-6
3
-1
-5
-9
0
-4
-8
-12

Figure 8.
The Dyck path
\pi=((1),(2,1))\in\mathrm{D}(4,3)
with the path
\tilde{\pi}
denoted in red. The set
\mathrm{Gaps}(\pi)=\{1,2\}
obtained from
(2,1)\setminus(1)
is highlighted in yellow and coincide in this case with the addable boxes, or co-generators, of
\pi
. The boxes corresponding to the
4
-generators of
\mathcal{A}(\pi)=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\mathrm{Gaps}(\pi)
are shaded green, the boxes corresponding to the
3
-generators are shaded blue. Note that
5
and
0
are both
3
and
4
-generators.

Theorem 3.18

(
[
24
]
)
.

The bijection
\mathcal{A}:\mathrm{D}(m,n)\to\mathrm{Inv}^{0}_{m,n}
is
\mathtt{area}
and
\mathtt{dinv}
preserving:

\mathtt{area}(\mathcal{A}(\pi))=\mathtt{area}(\pi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathtt{dinv}(\mathcal{A}(\pi))=\mathtt{dinv}_{n/m}(\pi).

We follow
[
24
,
27
]
and define the
(q,t)
-Catalan and Schröder polynomials as follows.

Definition 3.19
.

For any coprime
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the
rational
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomial

C_{m,n}(q,t)
is given by:

C_{m,n}(q,t):=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}}q^{\mathtt{area}(\Delta)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\Delta)}=(1-q)\sum_{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}}q^{\mathtt{area}(\Delta)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\Delta)}.

(3.2)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

\xi_{k}(\Delta):=|\{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\Delta)|n+k+1\leq x\leq k+n+m\}|.

(3.3)

Definition 3.20
.

For each coprime pair
(m,n)
, the
Schröder polynomial

S_{m,n}(q,t,a)
is:

S_{m,n}(q,t,a):=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}}q^{\mathtt{area}(\Delta)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\Delta)}\prod_{k\in\mathtt{cogen}(\Delta)}\left(1+at^{-\xi_{k}(\Delta)}\right).

(3.4)

It is evident that at
a=0
, the Schröder polynomial recovers the rational
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomial.

Remark 3.21
.

In the case when
m
and
n
are not relatively prime, the set
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
is infinite. Still, the same formulas (
3.2
) and (
3.4
) yield well-defined power series. In
[
27
]
the definition of the rational
(q,t)
-Catalan and -Schröder series include the non-relatively prime case. However, for the purposes of this paper, it is enough to consider the relatively prime case only.

Example 3.22
.

In Figure
9
we can see the bijection between the
4,3
-Dyck paths and the
0
-normalized
(4,3)
-invariant subsets, with the
the
\mathtt{area}
and
\mathtt{dinv}
statistics, and additional information to compute the Schröder polynomial explicitly computed in each case. Summing all the corresponding factors, we get the Schröder polynomial
S_{4,3}(q,t,a)
. Namely,

\displaystyle S_{4,3}(q,t,a)=

\displaystyle t^{3}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2})+qt^{2}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})+qt(1+a)(1+at^{-1})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +q^{2}t(1+a)(1+at^{-1})+q^{3}(1+a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{3}+q^{2}t+qt^{2}+t^{3}+qt+a(q^{3}+q^{2}t+qt^{2}+t^{3}+q^{2}+2qt+t^{2}+q+t)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +a^{2}(q^{2}+qt+t^{2}+q+t+1)+a^{3}.
