\mathtt{gen}=\{0,4,5\}
\mathtt{codinv}(\Delta)=2
\mathtt{dinv}(\Delta)=1

\mathtt{cogen}=\{1,2\}
(1+a)(1+at^{-1})

8
4
0
5
1
-3
2
-2
-6
-1
-5
-9
-4
-8
-12

\mathrm{Gaps}=\{1,2,5\}
\Delta=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\{1,2,5\}
\mathtt{area}(\Delta)=3

3
-
\mathtt{gen}=\{0,4,8\}
\mathtt{codinv}(\Delta)=3
\mathtt{dinv}(\Delta)=0

\mathtt{cogen}=\{5\}
(1+a)

Figure 9.
All
4,3
-Dyck paths are on the left, with the area boxes in yellow. The corresponding
(4,3)
-invariant subsets are in the second column, together with the
\mathtt{area}
,
3
-generators,
\mathtt{codinv},
and
\mathtt{dinv}
in the third column; the co-generators and contributing factors of
S_{4,3}(q,t,a)
are given in the fourth column.

3.4.
Recursions for Invariant Subsets

In
[
27
]
Gorsky-Mazin-Vazirani introduced certain power series indexed by invariant subsets and proved that they satisfy recursions similar to the Hogancamp-Mellit recursions
[
39
]
, thus providing an alternative model for computing the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of torus links. We now recall this construction.

For any sequence
{\bf w}=(w_{0},\dots,w_{m+n-1})\in\{0,1\}^{n+m}
, set

\mathrm{Inv}_{\bf w}:=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}\;|\;i\in\Delta\cap[0,n+m-1]\text{ if and only if }w_{i}=1\}.

(3.5)

A sequence
\bf w
is
admissible
if
\mathrm{Inv}_{\bf w}\neq\emptyset
.

Definition 3.23
.

For any
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}
, define the modified statistics:

\displaystyle\mathtt{area}^{\prime}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=|\overline{\Delta}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n+m}|,

\displaystyle\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\Delta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\Delta)}|\overline{\Delta}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n+m}\cap[x,x+m-1]|-{\binom{\xi_{-1}(\Delta)}{2}}.

In particular, for
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{0^{m+n}}
we have the identities:

\displaystyle\mathtt{area}^{\prime}(\Delta)=-(n+m)+\mathtt{area}(\Delta),

\displaystyle\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\Delta)=\mathtt{codinv}(\Delta).

Given a binary sequence
{\bf w}=(w_{0},\dots,w_{m+n-1})\in\{0,1\}^{m+n}
, we now construct two trinary sequences
{\bf x}\in\{0,1,\bullet\}^{m}
and
{\bf y}\in\{0,1,\bullet\}^{n}
such that,

-

{\bf x}
records gaps (by
0
),
n
-generators (by
1
), and any other element in
\Delta\cap[n,n+m-1]
(by
\bullet
), and

-

{\bf y}
records gaps (by
0
),
m
-generators (by
1
), and any other element in
\Delta\cap[m,n+m-1]
(by
\bullet
).

Definition 3.24
.

Given an admissible sequence
{\bf w}\in\{0,1\}^{m+n}
, define
{\bf x}=(x_{0},\dots x_{m-1})
and
{\bf y}=(y_{0},\dots y_{n-1})
by setting:

\displaystyle x_{i}:=\begin{cases}0&;w_{n+i}=0,\\
\bullet&;w_{n+i}=w_{i}=1,\\
1&;w_{n+i}=1\text{ and }w_{i}=0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{ and }y_{i}:=\begin{cases}0&;w_{m+i}=0,\\
\bullet&;w_{m+i}=w_{i}=1,\\
1&;w_{m+i}=1\text{ and }w_{i}=0.\end{cases}

We say a pair of sequences
{\bf x,y}
are admissible if and only if
{\bf w}
is admissible.

Example 3.25
.

Let
(m,n)=(7,4)
and
\Delta=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\{0,1,2,3,4,6,7,8,10\}.

Then, the associated binary sequence
w=00000100010
yields the ternary sequences
{\bf x}=01000\bullet 0
and
{\bf y}=0010
,
since the only
4
-generator in
[4,10]
is
5
and the only
7
-generator in
[7,10]
is
9
. In particular,
9
is not a
4
-generator since
9-4=5\in\Delta
. Thus,
\Delta\in I_{00000100010}=I_{01000\bullet 0,0010}
.

Definition 3.26
.

Given admissible trinary sequences
\bf x
and
\bf y
as above, set
\mathrm{Inv}_{\bf x,y}:=\mathrm{Inv}_{\bf w}
and let

Q_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(q,t,a):=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}}q^{\mathtt{area}^{\prime}(\Delta)}t^{-\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\Delta)}\prod_{k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\Delta)}(1+at^{-\xi_{k}(\Delta)}).

Note that one only considers non-negative co-generators in Definition
3.26
. However, if
\min(\Delta)\geq m+n-1\geq\min(m,n)
, then there are no negative co-generators and
\mathtt{cogen}(\Delta)=\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\Delta)
. In particular, one immediately obtains the following result.

Lemma 3.27
.

[
27
, Rem. 5.6]

The Schröder polynomial arises as a special case of
Q_{\bf x,y}
,

S_{m,n}(q,t,a)={(1-q)t^{\delta(m,n)}}Q_{0^{n},0^{n}}(q,t,a)=t^{\delta(m,n)}Q_{0^{m-1}1,0^{n-1}1}(q,t,a).

For any sequence
{\bf x}
of length
\ell
and
\star\in\{0,1,\bullet\}
, let
{\bf x}\star=(x_{0},\dots,x_{\ell},\star)
and
\star{\bf x}=(\star,x_{0},\dots,x_{\ell})
.

Theorem 3.28
.

[
27
, Thm. 4.10 and 4.13]

The following recursions hold:

\displaystyle Q_{0{\bf x},0{\bf y}}

\displaystyle=t^{-|{\bf x}|}Q_{{\bf x}1,{\bf y}1}+qt^{-|{\bf x}|}Q_{{\bf x}0,{\bf y}0},

\displaystyle Q_{1{\bf x},0{\bf y}}

\displaystyle=Q_{{\bf x}1,{\bf y}\bullet},

\displaystyle Q_{\bullet{\bf x},\bullet{\bf y}}

\displaystyle=Q_{{\bf x}\bullet,{\bf y}\bullet},

\displaystyle Q_{1{\bf x},1{\bf y}}

\displaystyle=(t^{|{\bf x}|}+a)Q_{{\bf x}\bullet,{\bf y}\bullet},

\displaystyle Q_{0{\bf x},1{\bf y}}

\displaystyle=Q_{{\bf x}\bullet,{\bf y}1},

\displaystyle Q_{\emptyset,\emptyset}

\displaystyle=1.

Thus, letting
(\bf{u,v})
be the sequences obtained from
(\bf{x,y})
by omitting all
\bullet
’s we have,

R_{{\bf u},{\bf v}}(q,t,a)=Q_{{\bf x},{\bf y}}(q,t,a).

Corollary 3.29

(
[
27
,
49
]
)
.

For any
m,n
coprime, the KR series of the
(m,n)
-torus knot is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{T(m,n)}=\frac{(at^{1/2}q^{-1/2})^{\delta(m,n)}}{1-q}Q_{0^{m-1}1,0^{n-1}1}(q,t,a)=\frac{(at^{-1/2}q^{-1/2})^{\delta(m,n)}}{1-q}S_{m,n}(q,t,a).

4.
Schröder Polynomials for Triangular Partitions

Generalized
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomials for triangular partitions were defined
[
6
]
, with more general combinatorial properties of such partitions studied by Bergeron-Mazin
[
5
]
. In this section, we extend many of the combinatorial results from §
3
and define the Schröder polynomial of an arbitrary triangular partition.

4.1.
Triangular Partitions Revisited

In order to define the Schröder polynomial for a triangular partition
\tau
, we need to relate
\tau
-Dyck paths to some invariant subsets in
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.
To achieve this, we first embed
\tau
inside an appropriate partition
\tau_{m,n}
for relatively prime integers
m
and
n.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\tau
be a triangular partition and let
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be positive relatively prime integers. We say that
\tau
is
(m,n)
-triangular
if there exists a pair of positive real numbers
(r,s)
such that
\tau=\tau_{r,s},

s/r=n/m,
and
s<n.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any triangular partition
\tau
, there exists a pair of positive relatively prime integers
(m,n)
such that
\tau
is
(m,n)
-triangular.

Proof.

It is easy to observe (see page 2 in
[
5
]
) that for any triangular partition
\tau
, there exists a line
L_{\tilde{r},\tilde{s}}=\{\tilde{r}y+\tilde{s}x=\tilde{s}\tilde{r}\}
such that
\tau=\tau_{\tilde{r},\tilde{s}}
and
L_{\tilde{r},\tilde{s}}
does not pass through any integer points. Then for any
(r,s)
sufficiently close to
(\tilde{r},\tilde{s})
, one also gets
\tau=\tau_{\tilde{r},\tilde{s}}=\tau_{r,s}.
In particular, one can additionally require that
s/r
is rational, and if
s/r=n/m
is the reduced form, i.e.
n
and
m
are positive relatively prime integers, then
s<n.
Indeed, the set of such pairs
(r,s)
is everywhere dense. We conclude that
\tau
is
(m,n)
-triangular.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

Equivalently, one could flip the perspective and begin with a pair of fixed positive relatively prime integers
(m,n)
. Then all the
(m,n)
-triangular partitions can be obtained by the following procedure. Consider the
(m,n)
-diagonal line
L_{m,n}=\{nx+my=nm\},
shift it down to
\{nx+my=z\}
for some
0<z<mn,
and take
(r,s)=(z/n,z/m),
so that
\{nx+my=z\}=L_{r,s}
and
\tau_{r,s}
is an
(m,n)
-triangular partition. By varying
0<z<mn
, one obtains all
(m,n)
-triangular partitions.

Definition 4.4
.

In the context of the proof of Lemma
4.2
above, we call the diagonal line
L_{r,s}=\{sx+ry=rs\}
the
shifted diagonal
and the diagonal line
L_{m,n}=\{mx+ny=mn\}
the
main diagonal
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
0<z<mn
and let
\ell=\lfloor z\rfloor.
Then
\tau_{z/n,z/m}=\tau_{\ell/n,\ell/m}.

Proof.

It suffices to show that a box
\Box=(i,j)
lies below
L_{z/n,z/m}
if and only if it lies below
L_{\ell/n,\ell/m}.
Indeed, since
i,j,m,n\in\mathbb{Z},

in+jm\leq z\Leftrightarrow in+jm\leq\lfloor z\rfloor=\ell.

∎

Corollary 4.6
.

For every triangular partition
\tau
, there exists a triple of positive integers
(m,n,\ell),
such that
m
and
n
are relatively prime,
\ell<mn,
and
\tau=\tau_{\ell/n,\ell/m}
is
(m,n)
-triangular.

Notation:
In what follows, the choice of main diagonal with respect to which a triangular partition
\tau_{r,s}
is considered will be very important. Thus, to make this choice explicit, all the combinatorial constructions will henceforth depend on the triple of integers
(m,n,\ell)
rather than the pair of real numbers
(r,s)
, using the triple to index the various objects. We will start by adopting the following notation whenever the triple
(m,n,\ell)
satisfies the conditions of Corollary
4.6
:

\displaystyle\tau_{m,n,\ell}

\displaystyle:=\tau_{\ell/n,\ell/m}=\tau_{r,s},

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{D}(\ell/n,\ell/m)=\mathrm{D}(r,s),

\displaystyle L_{m,n,\ell}

\displaystyle:=L_{\ell/n,\ell/m}=\{nx+my=\ell\}.

Remark 4.7
.

Note that the line
L_{m,n,\ell}
passes through the NE corner of the box
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times[n],
whose Anderson filling equals
\gamma(a,b)=mn-na-mb=mn-\ell.
If
b>0
, then
(a,b)\in\tau_{m,n,\ell}
and it is the box of
\tau_{m,n,\ell}
with the maximal Anderson filling. If
b\leq 0
, then all the labels in
\tau_{m,n,\ell}
are less then
mn-\ell.

4.2.
Triangular Schröder Polynomials

Let
\tau
be a triangular partition and let
(m,n,\ell)
be as in Corollary
4.6
, so that
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}.
In order to define triangular Schröder polynomials, we will first need to define a pair of trinary sequences
{\bf x}(m,n,\ell)\in\{0,1,\bullet\}^{m}
and
{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)\in\{0,1,\bullet\}^{n}.
We will later see that the associated Schröder polynomial actually depends only on the triangular partition
\tau
itself and not choice the triple
(m,n,\ell).

Recall the Anderson filling
\gamma(x,y)=\gamma_{m,n}(x,y)=mn-nx-my
from Definition
3.6
. Since for any such
(m,n,\ell)
we have
\tau_{m,n,\ell}\subseteq\tau_{m,n}
, then the pair
(\tau_{m,n,\ell},\tau_{m,n})
is an
m,n
-Dyck path as in Definition
3.3
.

In particular, we have

\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{m,n,\ell},\tau_{m,n})=\{\gamma(\Box)\;|\;\Box\in\tau_{m,n}\setminus\tau_{m,n,\ell}\}.

To each such Dyck path, we associate a particular subcollection of
0
-normalized
(m,n)
-invariant subsets
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
(Definition
3.14
).

Definition 4.8
.

For any triple
(m,n,\ell)
with
m
and
n
relatively prime and
\ell<mn
, set

\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}:=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}\;|\;\Delta\cap\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{m,n,l},\tau_{m,n})=\emptyset\}.

Recall the bijection
\mathcal{A}_{m,n}:\mathrm{D}(m,n)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}
mapping
(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})\mapsto\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\mathrm{Gaps}(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})
.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}:\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
be the map obtained via the natural restriction,

\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell}):=\mathcal{A}_{m,n}(\lambda,\tau_{m,n}).

We will soon see in Proposition
4.19
that the map
\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}:\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
is also a bijection.

Definition 4.10
.

For any triple
(m,n,\ell)
with
m
and
n
relatively prime and
\ell<mn
, define

\displaystyle W_{m,n,\ell}

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma(x,y)\;|\;\text{ the box $(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}\times[n]$ lies on the shifted diagonal $nx+my=l$}\},

\displaystyle U_{m,n,\ell}

\displaystyle:=\{\gamma(x,y)\;|\;\text{ the box $(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times[n]$ lies on the shifted diagonal $nx+my=l$}\},

and set

\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}:=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}\;|\;\Delta\cap W_{m,n,\ell}=U_{m,n,\ell}\}.

(4.1)

Remark 4.11
.

Recall from Lemma
3.8
that
\Gamma_{m,n}
can be identified with the set of Anderson labels
\gamma(x,y)
with
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times[n]
. Hence, we can write

U_{m,n,\ell}=\Gamma_{m,n}\cap W_{m,n,\ell}

and the condition defining
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
is
\Delta\cap W_{m,n,\ell}=\Gamma_{m,n}\cap W_{m,n,\ell}
.

Example 4.12
.

Consider the special case when
\ell=mn-1.
In this case,
W_{m,n,mn-1}=\{-m-n+1,-m-n+1,\ldots,0\}
and
U_{m,n,mn-1}=\{0\},
so that
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,mn-1}^{0}=\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}.
Moreover
\tau_{m,n,mn-1}=\tau_{m,n},
and one naturally recovers the case studied in
[
27
]
.

We now explicitly characterize
W_{m,n,\ell}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

The set
W_{m,n,\ell}
is given by the discrete interval
W_{m,n,\ell}=\big[\;mn-\ell-(m+n),mn-\ell-1\;\big]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Consider the set
S
consisting of boxes in
\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
lying on the shifted diagonal
L_{m,n,\ell}=\{nx+my=\ell\}
so that
W_{m,n,\ell}=\gamma(S)
. According to Definition
3.1
, a cell
(a,b)
is in
S
, if and only if

n(a-1)+m(b-1)\leq\ell<na+mb.

Hence,
S
consists precisely of cells
(a,b)
satisfying the condition that

\ell<na+mb\leq\ell+n+m.

Consequently, since
mn-\gamma(x,y)=nx+my
, then we see that
(a,b)\in S
if and only if

\ell<mn-\gamma(a,b)\leq\ell+n+m,

which holds if and only if

mn-\ell-(n+m)\leq\gamma(a,b)<mn-\ell.

Since by Lemma
3.8
the Anderson function is bijective onto
\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
, every value in this interval is achieved by
\gamma
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

All invariant sets in
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
have the same intersection with the interval
[0,mn-\ell-1]
. In fact, for any
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
, one has

\Delta\cap[0,mn-\ell-1]=\Gamma_{m,n}\cap[0,mn-\ell-1].

Proof.

Let
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
be arbitrary. Clearly,
\Gamma_{m,n}\subset\Delta
because
\Delta
contains
0
and is
m,n
-invariant. Suppose
k\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfies
0<k<mn-\ell
and
k\notin\Gamma_{m,n}
. We must show that
k\notin\Delta
.

Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
be the unique box such that
k=\gamma(a,b)
, i.e.
k=mn-(na+mb)
. The Anderson label being positive means
na+mb<mn
. Moreover,
a>0
since
k\notin\Gamma_{m,n}
, and
b>0
by assumption, so in fact
(a,b)\in\tau_{m,n}
.

The inequality
k<mn-\ell
is equivalent to
na+mb>\ell
, which implies
(a,b)\notin\tau_{m,n,\ell}
. Since
\Delta
is disjoint from
\gamma(\tau_{m,n}\setminus\tau_{m,n,\ell})
by definition of
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
, it follows that
(a,b)\notin\Delta
, as claimed.
∎

The next step is to characterize
\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
in terms of certain trinary sequences. Before doing so we introduce some notation. Recall that for any
A\subset\mathbb{Z}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
A+i=\{k\in\mathbb{Z}|k-i\in A\}
. Thus, given a subset
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})
of the power set of
\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\mathcal{S}+i:=\{A+i|A\in\mathcal{S}\},

\mathcal{S}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:=\{A\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}|A\in\mathcal{S}\}.

Definition 4.15
.

For any triple
(m,n,\ell)
with
m
and
n
relatively prime and
\ell<mn
, let

{\bf w}(m,n,\ell):=(w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{m+n-1})\in\{0,1\}^{m+n}

be the binary sequence defined such that
w_{i}=1
if and only if
i+mn-\ell-(n+m)\in\Gamma_{m,n}
(equivalently, if and only if
i+mn-\ell-(n+m)\in U_{m,n,\ell}
).

Furthermore, similar to Definition
3.24
, let
{\bf x}(m,n,\ell)=(x_{0},\dots x_{m-1})
and
{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)=(y_{0},\dots y_{n-1})
be given by:

x_{i}=\begin{cases}0&;w_{n+i}=0,\\
\bullet&;w_{n+i}=w_{i}=1,\\
1&;w_{n+i}=1\text{ and }w_{i}=0,\end{cases}\text{ and }\ \ y_{i}=\begin{cases}0&;w_{m+i}=0,\\
\bullet&;w_{m+i}=w_{i}=1,\\
1&;w_{m+i}=1\text{ and }w_{i}=0.\end{cases}

(4.2)

Finally, let
{\bf u}(m,n,\ell)
and
{\bf v}(m,n,\ell)
be the binary sequences obtained from
{\bf x}(m,n,\ell)
and
{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)
respectively by omitting all the
\bullet
’s.

Proposition 4.16
.

Retain the notation from Definition
4.15
. The map
\mathcal{I}:\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}\to\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}
sending

\Delta\mapsto(\Delta-(mn-\ell)+(n+m))\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

is a bijection. In particular,

\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}=(\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}-(mn-\ell)+(n+m))\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Proof.

By (
4.1
) we have that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inv}^{0}_{m,n,\ell}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}|\Delta\cap W_{m,n,\ell}=U_{m,n,\ell}\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}^{0}|j\in\Delta\cap W_{m,n,\ell}\Leftrightarrow j\in U_{m,n,\ell}\}.

Since by Lemma
4.13
we know that
W_{m,n,\ell}-(mn-\ell)+(n+m)=[0,m+n-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}
, it follows that

j\in\Delta\cap W_{m,n,\ell}
if and only if
j-(mn-\ell)+(n+m)\in(\Delta-(mn-\ell)+(n+m))\cap[0,m+n-1]
.

On the other hand, by (
3.5
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}\;|\;i\in\Delta^{\prime}\cap[0,m+n-1]\Leftrightarrow w_{i}=1\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n}|i\in\Delta^{\prime}\cap[0,m+n-1]\Leftrightarrow i+mn-\ell-(n+m)\in U_{m,n,\ell}\}.

Thus for any
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
, one gets
\mathcal{I}(\Delta)\in\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}.
Furthermore, by Lemma
4.14
, the inverse to
\mathcal{I}
is given by

\Delta^{\prime}\mapsto(\Delta^{\prime}+mn-\ell-(n+m))\cup\Gamma_{m,n}.

Therefore
\mathcal{I}:\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}\to\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}
is a bijection.
∎

The following Corollary follows immediately from definitions.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0},
then one has

\displaystyle\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{I}(\Delta))=\left(\mathtt{cogen}(\Delta)-(mn-\ell)+(m+n)\right)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{I}(\Delta))\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}=\left(\mathtt{ngen}(\Delta)-(mn-\ell)+(m+n)\right)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}.

Upon first inspection, it may not be entirely clear why the intersection with
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is needed. Indeed, whenever
mn-\ell-(n+m)\leq 0
such a restriction is unnecessary and in fact,

\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}=\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}-(mn-\ell)+(n+m).

(4.3)

However, when
mn-\ell-(n+m)>0
such a shift may generate negative values, which is not allowed since invariant subsets consist, by definition, of only nonnegative numbers.

We are finally ready to define the
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial for any triple
(m,n,\ell)
of positive integers, such that
m
and
n
are relatively prime and
\ell<mn.

Definition 4.18
.

The
triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomials
are defined as the family

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a):=t^{|\tau_{m,n,\ell}|}Q_{{\bf x}(m,n,\ell),{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)}(q,t,a).

In Theorem
5.19
we will prove that these polynomials depend only on the triangular partition
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
(and not on the choice of
m,n,l
), hence the term
triangular
is indeed well-suited.

Recall the map
\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}:\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
mapping
(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})\mapsto\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\mathrm{Gaps}(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})
and the bijection
\mathcal{I}:\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}\to\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}
sending
\Delta\mapsto(\Delta-(mn-\ell)+(n+m))\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
from Proposition
4.16
.

Proposition 4.19
.

The map
\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}:\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
is a bijection. Thus, the composition
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{I}\circ\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}:\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)}
sending

\displaystyle(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})\mapsto(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\setminus\mathrm{Gaps}(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})-(mn-\ell)+(n+m))\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

is also a bijection. Furthermore, for any
\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
we have

\mathtt{area}(\pi)=\mathtt{area}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)+\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=|\tau_{m,n,\ell}|.

Proof.

Suppose
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
and let
\hat{\pi}:=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})\in\mathrm{D}(m,n)
. Then
\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{m,n,\ell},\tau_{m,n})\subseteq\mathrm{Gaps}(\hat{\pi})
. Hence,

\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)\cap\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{m,n,\ell},\tau_{m,n})=(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\setminus\mathrm{Gaps}(\hat{\pi})\cap\mathrm{Gaps}(\tau_{m,n,\ell},\tau_{m,n})=\emptyset.

Thus,
\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)\in\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
. Since all the statements are reversible, then evidently
\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}:\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)\to\mathrm{Inv}_{m,n,\ell}^{0}
is indeed a bijection. Hence, combined with Proposition
4.16
, the second claim follows.

Now, by Definition
3.23
, one gets

\displaystyle\mathtt{area}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=

\displaystyle|\{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n+m}\;|\;k\notin\mathcal{D}(\pi)\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq mn-\ell}\;|\;k\notin\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq mn-\ell}\;|\;k\notin\Gamma_{m,n}\}\right|-\left|\{k\in\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)\;|\;k\notin\Gamma_{m,n}\}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\tau_{m,n,\ell}|-|\lambda|=\mathtt{area}(\pi).

Similarly, by Definition
3.23
one gets

\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=\sum_{x\;\in\;\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))}\left|\overline{\mathcal{D}(\pi)}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq m+n}\cap[x,x+m-1]\right|-\frac{\xi_{-1}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))(\xi_{-1}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))-1)}{2},

where

\displaystyle\xi_{-1}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=

\displaystyle\left|\{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))|n\leq x<n+m\}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))|mn-\ell-m\leq x<mn-\ell\}\right|=1,

since by Lemma
4.14
,

\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)\cap[0,mn-\ell-1]=\Gamma_{m,n}\cap[0,mn-\ell-1],

and
\mathtt{ngen}(\Gamma_{m,n})=\{0,m,2m,\ldots,(n-1)m\}.
Hence,

\displaystyle\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))}\left|\overline{\mathcal{D}(\pi)}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq m+n}\cap[x,x+m-1]\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))}\left|\overline{\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq mn-\ell}\cap[x,x+m-1]\right|.

Lemma
4.14
also implies that for every number
k\in[0,mn-\ell-1]
, there exists a unique
x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))
such that
k\in[x,x+m-1].
Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))}\left|\overline{\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cap[x,x+m-1]\right|-\left|\overline{\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)}\cap[0,mn-\ell-1]\right|

\displaystyle=\mathtt{codinv}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))-\left|\overline{\Gamma_{m,n}}\cap[0,mn-\ell-1]\right|

\displaystyle=\mathtt{codinv}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))-\left(\left|\overline{\Gamma_{m,n}}\right|-\left|\overline{\Gamma_{m,n}}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq mn-\ell}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{codinv}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))-|\tau_{m,n}|+|\tau_{m,n,\ell}|.

Concluding the proof, since from Definition
3.15
we have the relation

\mathtt{dinv}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))+\mathtt{codinv}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))=\delta(m,n)=|\tau_{m,n}|,

then combined with Theorem
3.18
we obtain the desired identity:

\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)+\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=\mathtt{dinv}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))+\mathtt{codinv}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))-|\tau_{m,n}|+|\tau_{m,n,\ell}|=|\tau_{m,n,\ell}|.

∎

One can use Proposition
4.19
to rewrite the triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
in term of the partition
\tau_{m,n,\ell}
, as follows:

\displaystyle S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)

\displaystyle=t^{|\tau|}Q_{{\bf x}(m,n,\ell),{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)}(q,t,a)

\displaystyle=t^{|\tau|}\sum_{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{{\bf x}(m,n,\ell),{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)}}q^{\mathtt{area}^{\prime}(\Delta)}t^{-\mathtt{codinv}(\Delta)}\prod_{k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\Delta)}(1+at^{-\xi_{k}(\Delta)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\prod_{k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi)))}(1+at^{-\xi_{k}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))}).

(4.4)

Corollary 4.20
.

The triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
specializes to the triangular
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomial of
[
5
,
6
]
at
a=0
,

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,0)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}=:C_{m,n,\ell}(q,t).

(4.5)

Remark 4.21
.

The triangular
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomials of
[
6
]
arise from the shuffle theorem under any line and thus depend only on the triangular partition
\tau
and not the choice of triple. Indeed, we will show both directly in Theorem
5.19
and as a consequence of Corollary
6.12
that
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
depends only on the partition and not the choice of triple, so that Corollary
4.20
is indeed consistent with
[
5
,
6
]
.

4.3.
A Schröder Path Reformulation

There is also a way to reformulate the elements of
\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))
and the statistics
\xi_{k}
in terms of the Dyck paths
\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
, which allows us to define
S_{m,n,\ell}
in terms of certain lattice paths with diagonal steps classically known as
Schröder paths
.

Definition 4.22
.

For an
m,n,\ell
-Dyck path
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})
, the
associated lattice path

\tilde{\pi}
is the same as the associated lattice path of the
m,n
-Dyck path
(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})
(Definition
3.3
). In other words,
\tilde{\pi}
is the south-east lattice path from
(0,n)
to
(m,0)
that follows the boundary of
\lambda.

Definition 4.23
.

Let
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
be a Dyck path and
\tilde{\pi}
be the associated lattice path. The
east boundary
of
\pi
is the set
\mathtt{EB}(\pi)
of boxes
\Box
in
\mathbb{Z}\times(0,n]
, such that the east boundary of
\Box
overlaps a vertical step of the path
\tilde{\pi}.
Let also
\mathtt{AB}(\pi)
denote the set of addable boxes of
\lambda
(Definition
3.17
). Finally, define the
shifted Anderson filling

\hat{\gamma}:(a,b)\mapsto\gamma(a,b)-(mn-\ell)+(n+m).

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
be an
m,n,\ell
-Dyck path. The shifted Anderson filling
\hat{\gamma}
provides a bijection between
\mathtt{AB}(\pi)
and
\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi)).
Furthermore, if
k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))
then

\xi_{k}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=\left|\{(a,b)\in\mathtt{EB}(\pi)|k+n<\hat{\gamma}(a,b)\leq k+n+m\}\right|.

Proof.

Let
k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))
be a non-negative co-generator and
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
be the unique box such that
\hat{\gamma}(a,b)=k
(similar to
\gamma,

\hat{\gamma}
also defines a bijection between
\mathbb{Z}\times[n]
and
\mathbb{Z}
).

Since
k\geq 0
and
k\notin\mathcal{D}(\pi)
it follows that

k-(n+m)+(mn-\ell)=\gamma(a,b)\notin\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi).

Similarly, since
k+n=\hat{\gamma}(a-1,b)\in\mathcal{D}(\pi)
and
k+m=\hat{\gamma}(a,b-1)\in\mathcal{D}(\pi),
it follows that

\gamma(a,b)+n=\gamma(a-1,b)\in\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)\text{ and }\gamma(a,b)+m=\gamma(a,b-1)\in\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi).

Therefore,
\gamma(a,b)\in\mathtt{cogen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)).
Recall that if
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{m,n,\ell})
then
\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)=\mathcal{A}_{m,n}(\lambda,\tau_{m,n})
and
\gamma
provides a bijection between
\mathtt{cogen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n}(\lambda,\tau_{m,n}))
and
\mathtt{AB}(\pi).

Reversing the argument, let
(a,b)\in\mathtt{AB}(\pi).
Then
\gamma(a,b)\in\mathtt{cogen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi)).
Furthermore, since the southwest corner of the box
(a,b)
lies below the shifted diagonal
L_{m,n,\ell}=\{nx+my=\ell\},
it follows that

\displaystyle n(a-1)+m(b-1)\leq\ell

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ \

\displaystyle mn-na-mb+(m+n)-(mn-\ell)\geq 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\ \

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}(a,b)\geq 0.

Hence, using Corollary
4.17
, we get

\hat{\gamma}(a,b)\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=\left(\mathtt{cogen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))-(mn-l)+(m+n)\right)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

By definition, for a non-negative co-generator
k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))
, one obtains

\displaystyle\xi_{k}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))=|\{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))|n+k+1\leq x\leq k+n+m\}|.

Since we only need to count
x\geq n+k+1\geq n,
applying Corollary
4.17
one gets

\displaystyle\xi_{k}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))

\displaystyle=|\{x\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}|n+k+1\leq x\leq k+n+m\}|

\displaystyle=|\{x\in\left(\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))-(mn-\ell)+(m+n)\right)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq n}|n+k+1\leq x\leq k+n+m\}|

\displaystyle=|\{x+(mn-\ell)-(m+n)\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))|n+k+1\leq x\leq k+n+m\}|.

Recall that
\gamma
provides a bijection between
\mathtt{EB}(\pi)
and
\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))
, and that
x+(mn-\ell)-(m+n)=\gamma(a,b)
if and only if
x=\hat{\gamma}(a,b).
Hence

\displaystyle\xi_{k}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))

\displaystyle=|\{x+(mn-\ell)-(m+n)\in\mathtt{ngen}(\mathcal{A}_{m,n,\ell}(\pi))|n+k+1\leq x\leq k+n+m\}|

\displaystyle=|\{(a,b)\in\mathtt{EB}(\pi)|k+n<\hat{\gamma}(a,b)\leq k+n+m\}|.

∎

Figure 10.
Displayed are the lattice path associated with the triangular partition
\lambda=(3,2)=\tau_{8,5,26}=\tau_{5.2,3.25}
in red, the Anderson labels with respect to
(m,n)=(8,5)
denoted in blue, the (dotted) main diagonal, and the (solid) shifted diagonal.

The above lemma motivates the following definition.

Definition 4.25
.

For any
\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
and addable box
\Box\in\mathtt{AB}(\pi)
, define:

\displaystyle\xi(\pi,\Box):=

\displaystyle\left|\{\Box^{\prime}\in\mathtt{EB}(\pi)|\hat{\gamma}(\Box)+n<\hat{\gamma}(\Box^{\prime})\leq\hat{\gamma}(\Box)+n+m\}\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\{\Box^{\prime}\in\mathtt{EB}(\pi)|\gamma(\Box)+n<\gamma(\Box^{\prime})\leq\gamma(\Box)+n+m\}\right|.

We can now given an expression for
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
in the language of
\tau_{m,n,\ell}
-Dyck paths.

Theorem 4.26
.

We have

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)=\sum_{k\geq 0}a^{k}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)\\
|L|=k\end{subarray}}t^{-\sum_{\Box\in L}\xi(\pi,\Box)}\right).

(4.6)

Proof.

Combining (
4.4
) and Lemma
4.24
we obtain,

\displaystyle S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\prod_{k\in\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{D}(\pi)))}\left(1+at^{-\xi_{k}(\mathcal{D}(\pi))}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\prod_{\Box\in\mathtt{AB}(\pi)}(1+at^{-\xi(\pi,\Box)}).

Expanding the product, we get the following expression.

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\sum_{L\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)}a^{|L|}t^{-\sum_{\Box\in L}\xi(\pi,\Box)}

Rearranging proves the Theorem.
∎

For each fixed
k
, the summation over the pairs
(\pi,L)
in (
4.6
) can be thought of as the summation over paths
\pi\in\mathrm{D}(m,n,\ell)
with exactly
k
marked addable boxes. One can also modify the marked addable boxes by replacing these with northwest diagonal steps. Lattice paths under the main diagonal consisting of north, west, and northwest diagonal steps are known as
Schröder paths

[
59
]
, hence the name of the polynomials
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a).
Schröder polynomials in the classical square case were introduced in
[
13
]
and
[
31
]
.

\hline\cr(2,1)&\Gamma_{8,5}\cup\{19,22,27\}&\{4,7,9,12\}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 13}\setminus\{13,16\}\\
\hline\cr(3)&\Gamma_{8,5}\cup\{17,22,27\}&\{4,7,9,12\}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 13}\setminus\{13,18\}\\
\hline\cr(2,2)&\Gamma_{8,5}\cup\{14,19,22,27\}&\{4,7,9,12\}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 13}\setminus\{16\}\\
\hline\cr(3,1)&\Gamma_{8,5}\cup\{17,19,22,27\}&\{4,7,9,12\}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 13}\setminus\{13\}\\
\hline\cr(3,2)&\Gamma_{8,5}\cup\{14,17,19,22,27\}&\{4,7,9,12\}\cup\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 13}\\
\end{array}

Figure 11.
The nine subparitions of
\tau_{8,5,26}
and their combinatorial data.

Example 4.27
.

Let
(m,n,\ell)=(8,5,26)
so that
\tau_{8,5,26}=\tau_{5.2,3.25}=(3,2)
. The partition
\tau_{8,5,26}
, Anderson labels, and shifted (solid) and main (dotted) diagonals can be seen in Figure
10
.

The set
W_{8,5,26}=[1,13]
(shaded yellow in Figure
10
) consists of the window of all boxes that lie on the shifted diagonal. Notice that since
U_{8,5,26}=\{5,8,10,13\}
and the gaps of
(\tau_{8,5,26},\tau_{8,5})
within
W_{8,5,26}
are precisely
\{1,2,3,4,6,7,9,11,12\}
, then:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inv}_{8,5,26}^{0}=

\displaystyle\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{8,5}^{0}:\{1,2,3,4,6,7,9,11,12\}\cap\Delta=\emptyset\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{8,5}^{0}:\Delta\cap[1,13]=\{5,8,10,13\}\}.

By Proposition
4.16
, we obtain

{\bf w}(8,5,26)=0000100101001,\ {\bf x}(8,5,26)=0010\bullet 00\bullet,\ \text{and}\ {\bf y}(8,5,26)=0100\bullet.

Hence,

\mathrm{Inv}_{0000100101001}=\mathrm{Inv}_{0010\bullet 00\bullet,0100\bullet}=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{8,5}\;|\;\Delta\cap[0,12]=\{4,7,9,12\}\}.

In particular, by directly comparing all the subsets in
I_{8,5,26}^{0}
and
I_{0000100101001}
corresponding to each of the nine subpartitions of
\tau_{8,5,26}=(3,2)
:
\emptyset,(1),(1,1),(2),(2,1),(3),(2,2),(3,1),
and
(3,2)
, we can see that indeed,
I_{0000100101001}
can be obtained from
I_{8,5,26}^{0}
by shifting each set down by one and then intersecting with
\mathbf{Z}_{\geq 0}
(thus discarding
-1
). See Figure
11
.

See Figure
12
for the computation of the contributions of the nine elements of
I_{0010\bullet 00\bullet,0100\bullet}
towards
Q_{0010\bullet 00\bullet,0100\bullet}(q,t,a)
. Putting it all together, we obtain

S_{8,5,26}(q,t,a)=t^{5}Q_{0010\bullet 00\bullet,0100\bullet}(q,t,a).

(4.7)

Expanded, this yields:

\displaystyle S_{8,5,26}(q,t,a)=

\displaystyle q^{5}(1+a)+q^{4}t(1+a)(1+at^{-1})+q^{3}t^{2}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle q^{3}t(1+a)(1+at^{-1})+q^{2}t^{3}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle q^{2}t^{2}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})+qt^{3}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle qt^{4}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2})+t^{5}(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2}).

\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}=\{8,10,11\}

\mathtt{area}^{\prime}=0

\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}=0,

\mathtt{dinv}=5

(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2})

Figure 12.
For the nine elements of
I_{0010\bullet 00\bullet,0100\bullet}
we record the gaps that are greater than or equal to
13,
since only those contribute to
\mathtt{area}^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}
. We also record the non-negative co-generators and the corresponding Schröder factors.

5.
The Knot Associated to a Triangular Partition

As we shall see, for
(m,n,\ell)
as in Section
4.1
, the Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
depends only on the triangular partition
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
cut by the diagonal line and not on the choice of triple. From a combinatorial perspective this is far from obvious (although it will also follow directly from the shuffle theorem under any path, see §
6
). In this section, we provide an independent and purely topological proof; namely, we associate a knot to each triple
(m,n,\ell)
and prove the following:

(1)

The KR series of
K_{\ell/n,\ell/m}
equals
S_{m,n,\ell}
(up to normalization).

(2)

Up to isotopy, the knot
K_{\ell/n,\ell/m}
depends only on the triangular partition
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
.

The construction of
K_{\ell/n,\ell/m}
uses a construction from Galashin-Lam
[
19
]
, and we refer the reader to that article for further details.

5.1.
Knots from Young Diagrams

\mapsto

Figure 13.
A primitive
(4,5)
curve
C
(left) and the square diagram representing the corresponding knot
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{C}
(right).

In this section we recall the notion of Coxeter knots. These generalize torus knots, and are particularly well-suited to connections with the flag Hilbert scheme
[
29
,
53
]
. For our purposes, it is most convenient to regard the family of (positive) Coxeter knots as parametrized by Young diagrams.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\tau
be a partition, and let
M
be an integer with
M>\tau_{1}
. Define a sequence
N>\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{M}=0
by letting
\mu_{j}
be the number of indices
i
with
\tau\geq j
(note that
\mu
is the transpose partition of
\tau
, extended by zero to obtain a sequence of length
M
).

Associated to the pair
(M,\tau)
, we have the braid
\beta^{cox}_{M,\tau}\in\mathrm{Br}_{M}
defined by

\beta^{cox}_{M,\tau}:=(\mathbbm{1}_{M-2}\sqcup\mathrm{JM}_{2})^{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}(\mathbbm{1}_{M-3}\sqcup\mathrm{JM}_{3})^{\mu_{2}-\mu_{3}}\dots\mathrm{JM}_{M}^{\mu_{M-1}-\mu_{M}}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{M-1},

(5.1)

where
\mathrm{JM}_{i}\in\mathrm{Br}_{i}
is the
Jucys-Murphy
braid
\mathrm{JM}_{i}=(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{i-1})(\sigma_{i-1}\cdots\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})
.
Knots which are closures of braids of this form are called
(positive) Coxeter knots
.

Proposition 5.2
.

The closure of
\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{M,\tau}
depends only on
\tau
up to isotopy. Furthermore, the normalizing exponent (
2.1
) is given by
\delta(\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{M,\tau})=|\tau|
.

Proof.

Increasing
M
by one has the following effect:
\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{M+1,\tau}=(\mathbbm{1}_{1}\sqcup\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{M,\tau})\sigma_{1}
, which represents the same knot as
\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{M,\tau}
. This proves the first statement.

For the second statement, note that the number of crossings in the braid (
5.1
) is

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=2(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})+4(\mu_{2}-\mu_{3})+\cdots+2M(\mu_{M-1}-0)+M-1

\displaystyle=2\mu_{1}+\cdots+2\mu_{M-1}+M-1

\displaystyle=2|\tau|+M-1,

since
\mu_{M}=0
.
The number of components is 1, so by definition we have

\delta(\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{M,\tau})=\frac{e+1-M}{2}=|\tau|,

as claimed.
∎

This proposition justifies the following definition.

Definition 5.3
.

Given any partition
\tau
and any integers
M,N\geq 1
with
\tau\subset[M-1]\times[N-1]
, we we will let
K_{\tau}
denote the Coxeter knot obtained as the closure of
\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{M,\tau}
, which is well-defined by Proposition
5.2
. In the special case when
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
is a triangular partition, we will write
K_{m,n,\ell}:=K_{\tau_{m,n,\ell}}
. Such knots will be called
broken torus knots
.

Remark 5.4
.

We chose the name
broken torus knot
to emphasize that
K_{\tau}
is obtained from a torus knot by removing an arc and re-gluing a different arc, as in Figures
13
and
5
.

As mentioned already, one of our main goals is to show that the Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
coincides with the the KR series of
K_{{m,n,\ell}}
. In order to prove this, we will need a more topologically friendly description of
K_{\tau}
as a monotone knot.

5.2.
Monotone Curves and Knots

Galashin-Lam
[
19
]
introduced the notion of a monotone knot and showed that the set of monotone knots (up to isotopy in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
) coincides with the set of positive Coxeter knots. In this section we recall their definition, taking some liberties.

Remark 5.5
.

The work of Galashin-Lam holds for links as well, but we will restrict to the case of knots for simplicity.

Let
M,N
be positive integers. A primitive
(M,N)
-
monotone curve

C
is the graph of a strictly decreasing continuous function
f:[0,M]\to[0,N]
satisfying
f(0)=N
,
f(M)=0
, and such that
C\cap\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}=\{(0,M),(N,0)\}
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
denote the torus and
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
be the quotient map. Then the
knot
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{C}
associated to a primitive monotone curve

C
is the knot
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{C}\subset\mathbb{T}\times[0,1]
defined to be concatenation of the curve
\{(\pi(x,f(x)),x/M)\>:\>x\in[0,M]\}
and the vertical line segment from
((0,0),1)
to
((0,0),0)
.

Let
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}_{C}
denote the image of
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{C}
under the standard embedding
\mathbb{T}\times[0,1]\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
. A knot
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
monotone
if it is isotopic to
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}_{C}
for some primitive monotone curve
C
.

Example 5.7
.

If
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
are coprime and
C
is the graph of
\{nx+my=mn\}
for
0\leq x\leq m
, then
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}_{C}
is the
(m,n)
torus knot. Thus, positive torus knots are monotone.

Below we setup some language for discussings knots in the thickened torus and the diagrams we use to represent them. In the definition below, note that for a surface
\Sigma
we will distinguish between knots in
\Sigma\times[0,1]
and their diagrams in
\Sigma
. Strictly speaking, a
knot diagram
for
K\subset\Sigma\times[0,1]
is an immersed curve
G
in
\Sigma
with double point singularities, together with overcrossing / undercrossing data at the double points. By a (mostly harmless) abuse, will write
G\subset\Sigma
and regard
G
as nothing more than a subset of
\Sigma
.

Figure 14.
A shift by
+(\epsilon,\epsilon)
of the curve
C
from Figure
13
, drawn in
\mathbb{R}^{2}/(4\mathbb{Z}\times 5\mathbb{Z})
(left), and the corresponding shifted square diagram
G^{I\times I}_{+}
(right).

Definition 5.8
.

Throughout this definition, let
I:=[0,1]
. Recall that we identify
\mathbb{T}
as
\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}
or, equivalently, as the unit square
[0,1]^{2}
with opposite edges identified. The image of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(respectively
\{0,1\}^{2}
) in
\mathbb{T}
will be referred to as the
basepoint of
\mathbb{T}
.

Let
K^{\mathbb{T}}
be a knot (or link) in
\mathbb{T}\times I
. A
square diagram
for
K^{\mathbb{T}}
is a diagram (with boundary)
G^{I\times I}
in the unit square
I\times I
which becomes a knot diagram for
K^{\mathbb{T}}
after identifying opposite edges.

Note that all printed diagrams of knots in the thickened torus are square diagrams in the above sense.

Remark 5.9
.

To obtain a square diagram for a monotone knot
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{C}
, we draw
G^{I\times I}
so that for two arcs
\alpha=C\cap([i-1,i]\times[j-1,j])
and
\alpha^{\prime}=C\cap([i^{\prime}-1,i^{\prime}]\times[j^{\prime}-1,j^{\prime}])
with
i<i^{\prime}
, the image of
\alpha^{\prime}
in
[0,1]^{2}
is drawn
above
the image of
\alpha
. See Figure
13
for an example.

Construction 5.10
.

Let
K^{\mathbb{T}}\subset\mathbb{T}\times I
be a knot (or link) in the thickened torus, and let
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
denote its image under the standard embedding
\mathbb{T}\times I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let
G^{\mathbb{T}}\subset\mathbb{T}
be a knot diagram for
K^{\mathbb{T}}
, and let
G^{I\times I}
be the preimage of
G^{\mathbb{T}}
under the projection
I^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
, so that
G^{I\times I}
is a square diagram for
K^{\mathbb{T}}
. We describe how to obtain a diagram for
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
from
G^{I\times I}

Assume that
G^{\mathbb{T}}
is disjoint from the basepoint (if not, see below), so that
G^{I\times I}
is disjoint from the corners
\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}
. The boundary of
G^{I\times I}
will consist of pairs of matching points of the form
(X\times\{0,1\})\ \sqcup(\{0,1\}\times Y)
, where
X,Y
are finite subsets of
(0,1)
. To obtain a diagram of
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
from this
G
, we connect matching points
\{x\}\times\{0,1\}
by arcs below the rest of the diagram, and we connect matching points
\{0,1\}\times\{y\}
by any disjoint family of arcs in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus[0,1]^{2}
. The result is a knot diagram, denoted
G^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}
, which represents
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
. See Figure
15
for an example.

If
G^{\mathbb{T}}
meets the basepoint, then we let
G^{\mathbb{T}}_{\pm}
denote the diagrams obtained by adding
\pm(\epsilon,\epsilon)
for
\epsilon>0
small, we let
G^{I\times I}_{\pm}
denote their lifts to the unit square, and we let
G^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}_{\pm}
the diagrams for
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
obtained as above.

Figure 15.
The knot diagram
G^{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}_{+}
obtained by closing up the shifted square diagram
G^{I\times I}_{+}
from Figure
14
, as in Construction
5.10
. The orange coloring will play a role when we compare to Figure
5
, and may be ignored for now.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
C
be a primitive
(M,N)
-monotone curve
C
. Let
\tau_{C}
denote the maximal partition
contained in
\{0\leq y\leq f(x)\>|\>0\leq x\leq M\}
.

In other words, a box
(x,y)\in[m]\times[n]
is in
\tau_{C}
if and only if
y<f(x)
. The connection between monotone knots and Coxeter knots is provided by the following.

We refer the reader to
[
19
, Prop. 7.5]
for a restatement and proof of the following fact.

Proposition 5.12
.

We have
K^{\mathbb{R}^{3}}_{C}=K_{\tau_{C}}
, where
K_{\tau_{C}}
is as in Definition
5.1
.
In particular, all monotone knots are Coxeter knots, and conversely.

We note a few very important special cases of
K_{\tau}
.

Example 5.13
.

Let
m,n\geq 1
be coprime integers, and set
\tau=\tau_{m,n}
. Then
K_{\tau}=T(m,n)
is the
(m,n)
-torus knot. We may present
T(m,n)
in the standard way, as the monotone knot associated to the straight line segment
C
from
(0,n)
to
(m,0)
. Alternatively, we may deform
C
by an isotopy in
[0,m]\times[0,n]\setminus\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, obtaining various non-standard presentations.

The associated sequence
N>\mu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{M}=0
is defined by
\mu_{i}=\lfloor n-\frac{in}{m}\rfloor=\lfloor\frac{(m-i)n}{m}\rfloor
, and from Proposition
5.12
one obtains a description of
T(m,n)
as the closure of the braid

\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{m,\tau}=\mathrm{JM}_{2}^{\lfloor\frac{(m-1)n}{m}\rfloor-\lfloor\frac{(m-2)n}{m}\rfloor}\mathrm{JM}_{3}^{\lfloor\frac{(m-2)n}{m}\rfloor-\lfloor\frac{(m-3)n}{m}\rfloor}\cdots\mathrm{JM}_{m}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\rfloor-0}\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m-1},

(where we are writing
\mathrm{JM}_{i}=\mathbbm{1}_{M-i}\sqcup\mathrm{JM}_{i}
, by a mild abuse of notation). This braid representing
T(m,n)
also appears, for instance, in
[
29
, Conjecture 3.28]
.

Example 5.14
.

Let
m,n,d\geq 1
be integers with
m,n
coprime, and set
\tau=\tau_{md,nd}
. Then
K_{\tau}
is the
(d,mnd+1)
cable of
T(m,n)
torus knot. This was proven by Galashin and Lam (
[
19
, Lemma 8.1]
). We give an alternate proof in Appendix
B
.

5.3.
Knots from Binary Sequences; Redux

In this section we show that every knot
K_{\tau}
with
\tau
a triangular partition is of the form
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
, for some pair of binary sequences (and conversely). The notion of monotone knots (and the flexibility afforded by being able to perform isotopies in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) will help facilitate this connection. To do so, we begin by first reconstructing the trinary sequences
{\bf x}(m,n,\ell)
and
{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)
from (
4.2
).

Figure 16.
We continue the example depicted in Figures
13
and
14
. In order to facilitate the Coxeter knot description
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{C}
we perform an isotopy and shift by
-(\epsilon,\epsilon)
(instead of
+(\epsilon,\epsilon)
as we did in Figure
14
). Closing up as in Construction
5.10
yields a diagram which is readily seen to be equivalent to the the closure of
\beta^{\mathrm{cox}}_{(2,1,1)}
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
(m,n)
be positive relatively prime integers and
0<\ell<mn,

\ell\in\mathbb{Z}.
Consider the corresponding trinary sequences
{\bf x}(m,n,\ell)=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{m-1})
and
{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)=(y_{0},\ldots,y_{n-1}).
Then,

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }i\equiv\ell\text{ modulo }m\\
0&\text{ if }i\equiv\ell-nx\text{ modulo }m\text{ for some }x\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor\}\\
\bullet&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle y_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }j\equiv\ell\text{ modulo }n\\
0&\text{ if }j\equiv\ell-my\text{ modulo }n\text{ for some }y\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\ell/m\rfloor\}\\
\bullet&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall that the binary sequence
{\bf w}(m,n,\ell)
from Definition
4.15
is given by the condition that
w_{i}(m,n,\ell)=1
if and only if

i+mn-\ell-(n+m)\in\Gamma_{m,n}.

By the definition of
{\bf x}(m,n,\ell)
one gets
x_{i}=1
if and only if
w_{i+n}=1
and
w_{i}=0,
which is equivalent to
i+mn-\ell-(n+m)\notin\Gamma_{m,n}
and
i+mn-\ell-m\in\Gamma_{m,n}.
In other words,
i+mn-\ell-m
is an
n
-generator of
\Gamma_{m,n}.
It is not hard to see that the set of
n
-generators of
\Gamma_{m,n}
is
\{0,m,2m,\ldots,(n-1)m\}.
Since for
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,m-1\}
and
\ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,mn-1\}
one gets
-m<i+mn-\ell-m<mn,
we conclude that
x_{i}=1
if and only if
i+mn-\ell-m
is divisible by
m,
which is equivalent to
i\equiv\ell
modulo
m.

Similarly,
x_{i}=0
if and only if
w_{i+n}=0,
or
i+mn-\ell-m\notin\Gamma_{m,n}.
It is well known that the complement to the semigroup
\Gamma_{m,n}
is given by

\mathbb{Z}\setminus\Gamma_{m,n}=mn-(m+n)-\Gamma_{m,n}.

Therefore, one obtains that
i+mn-\ell-m\notin\Gamma_{m,n}
if and only if

mn-(m+n)-i-mn+\ell+m=\ell-n-i\in\Gamma_{m,n}.

Equivalently,
x_{i}=0
if and only if
i
is the remainder modulo
m
of
\ell-nx
, for some
x\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor\}.

The case for
{\bf y}(m,n,\ell)
follows directly by exchanging
m
and
n
in the argument above.

∎

Example 5.16
.

Recall Example
4.12
. Then in that case, for
\ell=mn-1,
we have:

\mathbf{x}(m,n,mn-1)=(0^{m-1}1)\text{ and }\mathbf{y}(m,n,mn-1)=(0^{n-1}1).

Construction 5.17
.

Given any triangular partition
\tau
, choose a triple
(m,n,\ell)
with
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
. As in §
4.2
let
\mathbf{u}(m,n,\ell)\in\{0,1\}^{m^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{v}(m,n,\ell)\in\{0,1\}^{n^{\prime}}
be the binary sequences obtained from
\mathbf{x}(m,n,\ell)\in\{0,1,\bullet\}^{m}
and
\mathbf{y}(m,n,\ell)\in\{0,1,\bullet\}^{n}
in Lemma
5.15
by deleting the occurences of
\bullet
, where
m^{\prime}=\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor+1
and
n^{\prime}=\lfloor\ell/m\rfloor+1
.

Lemma 5.18
.

For any triangular partition
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
, we have

K_{\tau}=K_{\mathbf{u}(m,n,\ell),\mathbf{v}(m,n,\ell)}.

Conversely, given
\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{m^{\prime}}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{n^{\prime}}
with
|\mathbf{u}|=1=|\mathbf{v}|
such that
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
is a knot, we have
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}=K_{\tau}
, for some triangular partition.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will denote the image of
x\in\mathbb{R}
under the quotient
\mathbb{R}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
by
x+\mathbb{Z}
. Then points in the 2d torus
\mathbb{T}
are pairs
(x+\mathbb{Z},y+\mathbb{Z})
.

Fix a triple
(m,n,\ell)
with
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
, and let
M=\left\lceil\frac{\ell}{n}\right\rceil
and
N=\left\lceil\frac{\ell}{m}\right\rceil
. Choose
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
to be as small and generic as needed (in a sense that will be made precise in a moment) and let
r:=\frac{\ell+\epsilon}{n}
and
s:=\frac{\ell+\epsilon}{m}
. Let
C
be the piecewise linear curve
C=C_{\infty}\cup C_{n/m}\cup C_{0}
from
(0,N)
to
(0,s)
to
(r,0)
to
(M,0)
. Assume
\epsilon
is generic and small enough so that
C
is primitive, and
\tau=\tau_{C}
.

Let

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=\Big\{x\in(0,1)\>\Big|\>x\equiv r-\frac{jm}{n}\text{ (mod $\mathbb{Z}$)}\text{ with }0\leq j\leq\ell/m\Big\},

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle:=\Big\{y\in(0,1)\>\Big|\>y\equiv s-\frac{in}{m}\text{ (mod $\mathbb{Z}$)}\text{ with }0\leq i\leq\ell/n\Big\}.

Note that each element of
X
is the
x
-coordinate (mod
\mathbb{Z}
) of a point of intersection between
C_{n/m}
and
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
. Similarly, each element of
Y
is the
y
-coordinate (mod
\mathbb{Z}
) of a point of intersection between
C_{n/m}
and
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
.

The number of elements of
X
is the number of integers
j
with
0\leq mj\leq\ell+\epsilon
. Since
\epsilon
is arbitrarily small, this is equivalent to
0\leq mj\leq\ell
. Thus,
|X|=n^{\prime}=\lfloor\ell/m\rfloor+1
. Similarly,
|Y|=m^{\prime}:=\lfloor\ell/n\rfloor+1
.

Using the natural order on the interval
(0,1)
, we order the points in
X
and
Y
, writing them as
X=\{x_{1}<\cdots<x_{n^{\prime}}\}
and
Y=\{y_{1}<\cdots<y_{m^{\prime}}\}
.

Let
a\in\{1,\ldots,m^{\prime}\}
and
b\in\{1,\ldots,n^{\prime}\}
be the indices such that
y_{a}\equiv s
(mod
\mathbb{Z}
) and
x_{b}\equiv r
(mod
\mathbb{Z}
). Now, define binary sequences
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m^{\prime}})\in\{0,1\}^{m^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{m^{\prime}})\in\{0,1\}^{n^{\prime}}
by
u_{a}=1=v_{b}
, and
u_{i}=0=v_{j}
for all indices
i\neq a
,
j\neq b
. Lemma
5.15
tells us that
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}(m,n,\ell)
and
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}(m,n,\ell)
.

Now, consider the Construction
2.20
, which tells us how to draw a diagram for
K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
. In that construction, with the above choices of
x_{j}
and
y_{j}
, the union of all arcs of the form
A_{k},H_{i},V_{j}
coincides exactly with the contribution of
C_{n/m}
to the monotone knot
K_{C}=K_{C_{\infty}\cup C_{n/m}\cup C_{0}}
.

It remains only to observe that
B=B_{-}\cup B_{+}
(shown in orange in Figure
5
) is isotopic to the contribution of
C_{\infty}
and
C_{0}
to
K_{C}
(shown in orange in Figure
15
).

For the converse, let
K=K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}
be given, with
\mathbf{u}\in\{0,1\}^{m^{\prime}}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{n^{\prime}}
. Let
C^{\prime}
denote the union of all arcs of the form
A_{k},H_{i},V_{j}
from Construction
2.20
, so that
K=C^{\prime}\cup B
. We may regard
C^{\prime}
as embedded in the standard 2d torus
\mathbb{T}
. Since
C^{\prime}
is embedded in
\mathbb{T}
, we may regard
C^{\prime}
as a piece of a torus knot
T(m,n)
, for some
m,n
coprime. More precisely, up to an isotopy we can arrange that
C^{\prime}
be the image of a straight line segment from
(0,s)
to
(r,0)
, for some real numbers
r,s
with
s/r=n/m
and
\lfloor r\rfloor+1=m^{\prime}
, and
\lfloor s\rfloor+1=n^{\prime}
(these last two equations come from counting the number of intersections of
C^{\prime}
with the meridian and longitude of
\mathbb{T}
). The remaining arc
B
is isotopic to the image of line segments from
(0,n^{\prime})
to
(0,s)
and from
(r,0)
to
(m^{\prime},0)
, as we saw above.
Thus
K
is a broken torus knot.
∎

5.4.
The Invariance Theorem for
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)

We are finally ready to state and prove our theorem on the independence of the Schröder polynomials
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
on the choice of triple.

Theorem 5.19
.

For any triangular partition
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
, the corresponding Schröder polynomial
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
equals the KR series of the knot
K_{\tau}
, up to normalization:

S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)=(1-q)(at^{-1/2}q^{-1/2})^{-|\tau|}P_{K_{\tau}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a).

In particular,
S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a)
depends only on the triangular partition
\tau
.

Proof.

Let
m,n,\ell
and
\tau=\tau_{m,n,\ell}
be as in the statement. Abbreviate by writing
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}(m,n,\ell)
and
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}(m,n,\ell)
. Lemma
5.18
gives us that

H_{\mathrm{KR}}(K_{\tau})=H_{\mathrm{KR}}(K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}).

Using Proposition
5.2
to compute the appropriate normalizing factor
\delta(\beta)
, we observe the following

\mathcal{P}_{K_{\tau}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a)=\mathcal{P}_{K_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}}^{\text{KR}}(q,t,a)=\frac{(at^{-1/2}q^{-1/2})^{|\tau|}}{1-q}t^{|\tau|}R_{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}}(q,t,a)=\frac{(at^{-1/2}q^{-1/2})^{|\tau|}}{1-q}S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a),

as claimed. In the first equality we used Lemma
5.18
, in the second we used Theorem
2.23
, and in the final equality we used Theorem
3.28
.
∎

The above theorem justifies the following definition.

Definition 5.20
.

For
\tau
a triangular partition, we define

S_{\tau}(q,t,a):=S_{m,n,\ell}(q,t,a),

(5.2)

where
(m,n,\ell)
is any triple as in Section
4.1
for which
\tau_{m,n,\ell}=\tau
.

Thus, as discussed in Remark
4.21
, specializing at
a=0
yields the triangular
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomials of
[
5
,
6
]
:

S_{\tau}(q,t,0)=C_{\tau}(q,t).

15
