The triangular partition
\tau=(3,2)=\tau_{5,4,18}=\tau_{18/4,18/5}=\tau_{8,5,26}
and the Anderson labels denoted in blue (left) and the same diagram with the labels shifted up by
7
(right).

Example 5.21
.

Consider
(m,n,\ell)=(5,4,18)
and notice that
\tau_{5,4,18}=(3,2)=\tau_{8,5,26}
(See Example
4.27
). Then,
W_{5,4,18}=[-7,1]
so that
{\bf w}(5,4,18)=000000010
and
{\bf x}(5,4,18)=00010
,
{\bf y}(5,4,18)=0010
. As pictured in Figure
17
, shifting the sets in
\mathrm{Inv}_{5,4,18}
does not generate negative numbers, hence:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Inv}_{00010,0010}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{5,4}\;|\;\Delta\in(\mathrm{Inv}_{5,4,18}^{0}+7)\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}

\displaystyle=\{\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{5,4}\;|\;\Delta\in\mathrm{Inv}_{5,4,18}^{0}+7\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Inv}_{5,4,18}^{0}+7.

Hence, the nine subdiagrams of
\tau_{5,4,18}
give rise to the following sets in
\mathrm{Inv}_{00010,0010}
:

\begin{array}[]{lllccl}&\underline{\mathtt{cogen}_{\geq 0}}&\underline{4-\mathtt{gen}}&\underline{\mathtt{area}^{\prime}}&\underline{\mathtt{codinv}^{\prime}}&\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,9,10,13,14,18\}&\{18\}&\{7,12,17,22\}&5&5&(1+a)\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,9,10,13,14\}&\{13,14\}&\{7,12,17,18\}&4&4&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,9,10,13\}&\{10,13\}&\{7,12,14,17\}&3&3&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,9,10,14\}&\{8,14\}&\{7,12,13,18\}&3&4&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,9,10\}&\{8,9,10\}&\{7,12,13,14\}&2&2&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2})\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,9,13\}&\{6,13\}&\{7,10,12,17\}&2&3&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,10\}&\{8,10\}&\{7,9,12,14\}&1&2&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8,9\}&\{6,8,9\}&\{7,10,12,13\}&1&1&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2})\\
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 7}\setminus\{8\}&\{5,6,8\}&\{7,9,10,12\}&0&0&(1+a)(1+at^{-1})(1+at^{-2}).\end{array}

A straightforward computation confirms that
Q_{00010,0010}(q,t,a)=Q_{01000\bullet 0,0010}(q,t,a)
. Thus, comparing with Example
4.27
, we indeed obtain that

S_{(3,2)}(q,t,a)=S_{5,4,18}(q,t,a)=S_{8,5,26}(q,t,a).

In particular, specializing at
a=0
yields the triangular
(q,t)
-Catalan polynomial:

\displaystyle C_{(3,2)}(q,t)=S_{(3,2)}(q,t,0)=q^{5}+q^{4}t+q^{3}t^{2}+q^{3}t+q^{2}t^{3}+q^{2}t^{2}+qt^{3}+qt^{4}+t^{5}.

5.5.
Compactified Jacobians and the Oblomkov-Rasmussen-Shende Conjecture

Let
Z\subset\mathbf{C}^{2}
be a complex curve given by an analytic equation
f(x,y)=0.
Assume that
(0,0)\in Z.
It is well known that for a small enough
\epsilon>0
, the sphere
S_{\epsilon}:=\{\|(x,y)\|=\epsilon\}
intersects
Z
transversely. The intersection is a smooth compact real curve inside the three dimensional sphere
S_{\epsilon},
in other words, a link. The links that appear this way are called
algebraic links
. The study of algebraic links goes back almost a century to the works of Brauner
[
7
]
and Kähler
[
40
]
. For a more modern treatment of the subject, see
[
14
]
.

In the last several decades, there has been considerable interest in relating various invariants of the planar curve singularities to the invariants of the corresponding algebraic links. In
[
52
]
Oblomkov, Rasmussen, and Shende conjectured a formula expressing the Poincaré series of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of an algebraic link in terms of the weight polynomials of the Hilbert schemes of points of the singularity. Migliorini and Shende
[
50
]
, and independently Maulik and Yun
[
46
]
, related the cohomology of the Hilbert scheme of a singular complex curve to the cohomology of its
compactified Jacobian
equipped with a certain canonical filtration, called the
perverse filtration
. Combined with these results, the
Oblomkov-Rasmussen-Shende conjecture
predicts that the minimal
a
-degree part of the Poincaré series of the Khovanov–Rozansky homology of a link of a singularity can be explicitly recovered from the cohomology of the compactified Jacobian of the singularity, equipped with the perverse filtration. The perverse filtration, which is responsible for the ‘
q
’ degree, is notoriously hard to compute. For this reason, it is simpler to forget the perverse filtration and specialize
q=1
. However, Khovanov–Rozansky series have denominators of the form
\frac{1}{1-q}
, which must be cleared before specializing. The modified version of the Oblomkov-Rasmussen-Shende conjecture is stated below.

Conjecture 5.22
.

Let
Z=\{f(x,y)=0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
be a curve with a unibranch singularity at
0
, and let
K=K_{Z}
denote its link (actually a knot since
0\in Z
is a unibranch singularity). Let
\delta
be the Milnor number of
0\in Z
,
\overline{JC}
denote its compactified Jacobian, and let
\mathcal{P}_{\overline{JC}}(u)
be its Poincaré polynomial. Then

\Big((1-q)\mathcal{P}^{\text{KR}}_{K}(q,t,a)\Big)|_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
t=u^{-2}\end{subarray}}=a^{\delta}u^{-\delta}\mathcal{P}_{\overline{JC}}(u)+(\text{terms with $a$-degree $>\delta$}).

We refer the reader to
[
24
,
50
,
46
,
52
]
and the references therein for the definitions of compactified Jacobians, Hilbert schemes, perverse filtrations, and all the relevant constructions.

Recently, in
[
25
]
, the fourth author together with Gorsky and Oblomkov studied the compactified Jacobians of the planar curves that can be parametrized by

(x(t),y(t))=(t^{nd},t^{md}+At^{md+1}+\dots),

(5.3)

where
n
and
m
are positive relatively prime integers,
d
is an integer greater than
1,
and
A\neq 0
is a non-zero complex number. They proved that the compactified Jacobian in this case admits an affine paving with the cells enumerated by the
dm,dn
-Dyck paths. Assembling their computation with some well-known facts in conjunction with our Theorem
5.19
, establishes the following.

Theorem 5.23
.

Given integers
m,n,d\geq 1
with
m,n
coprime, let
Z
be the curve with parametrization as in (
5.3
), and let
\overline{JC}
be the compactified Jacobian of the singularity
0\in Z
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{\overline{JC}}(u)
be its Poincaré polynomial. Then:

(1)

the Milnor number of
0\in Z
is
\delta=\delta(md,nd)
,

(2)

the link of
0\in Z
is
K_{\tau_{md,nd}}
, which equals the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of
T(m,n)
,

(3)

Conjecture
5.22
is true for the
(d,mnd+1)
-cable of
T(m,n)
.

Proof.

Statement (1) is well-known. The fact that the link of
0\in Z
is the
(d,dnm+1)
-cable of the torus knot
T(m,n)
is well-known (see
[
14
]
). The fact that this knot coincides with
\tau_{md,nd}
was first established by Galashin-Lam
[
19
]
, but it also follows from Lemma
5.18
.

From statement (2), we have the computation (letting
\tau=\tau_{md,nd}
) from Theorem
5.19

\Big((1-q)\mathcal{P}_{K_{\tau}}(q,t,a)\Big)|_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=1\\
t=u^{-2}\end{subarray}}=(au)^{\delta}C_{\tau}(1,u^{-2})+(\text{h.o.t.}),

where
C_{\tau}(q,t)=S_{\tau}(q,t,0)
as in Corollary
4.20
. Comparing this with the computation in
[
25
, Theorem 1.4]

\mathcal{P}_{\overline{JC}}(u)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(md,nd)}u^{2\mathtt{codinv}(\pi)}=u^{2\delta}C_{\tau}(1,u^{-2}),

proves statement (3).
∎

6.
A
(q,t)
-Schröder Theorem for Paths Under Any Line

The
classical shuffle theorem
, conjectured by Haglund et al
[
34
]
and proven by Carlsson and Mellit
[
9
]
, gives a combinatorial formula in terms of
(n,n+1)
-Dyck paths for the action of the operator
\nabla
on the
n^{th}
elementary symmetric function
e_{n}
, which had previously been proven by Haiman
[
35
,
36
]
to equal the Frobenius character of the ring of diagonal coinvariants. It was conjectured by Egge et. al.
[
13
]
and proven by Haglund
[
31
]
that the
(q,t)
-Schröder theorem
holds,

S_{n,n+1}(q,t,a)=\sum_{k\geq 0}S_{n,k}(q,t)a^{k}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\langle\nabla e_{n},h_{k}e_{n-k}\rangle\;a^{k},

where
S_{n,k}
are the classical Schröder polynomials defined in terms of so-called Schröder paths
[
59
,
13
]
.

More recently, building upon the proof of the shuffle theorem and its rational generalization
[
28
,
48
]
, Blasiak-Haiman-Morse-Pun-Seelinger
[
6
]
proved a vast generalization of the shuffle theorem for Dyck paths under lines of any real slope. In what follows we prove a complete generalization of the
(q,t)
-Schröder Theorem by showing that our triangular Schröder polynomial
S_{\tau}(q,t,a)
arises as the sum of the hook components of the shuffle theorem under any line. Namely, we will prove that

S_{\tau}(q,t,a)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(X;q,t),h_{k}e_{\lfloor s\rfloor-k}\rangle\;a^{k},

(6.1)

where
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}(X;q,t)
is the symmetric function obtained by the action of particular elliptic Hall algebra elements indexed by a triangular partition
\tau
on
1
.

6.1.
The Shuffle Theorem Under Any Line

We begin by recalling some essential definitions from
[
6
]
and refer the reader to this source for additional details.

Let
\nu=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{\ell})
be a
\ell
-tuple of skew diagrams. For a fixed
\epsilon>0
such that
\ell\epsilon<1
, we define the
content
of a cell
\Box\in\nu_{j}
with northeast corner
(x,y)
to be
c(\Box)=x-y+j\epsilon
. A
reading order
on
\nu
is any total ordering of the boxes of
\nu
in which
c
is weakly increasing. Two cells
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
form an
attacking pair
if
0<|c(\Box_{1})-c(\Box_{2})|<1
. Let

\mathrm{I}(T):=\#\text{attacking pairs of }\nu.

As usual, we denote by
\mathrm{SSYT}(\nu)
and
\mathrm{SYT}(\nu)
the sets of semistandard and standard Young tableaux of shape
\nu
.

Given a semistandard filling
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\nu)
with entries in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we say an attacking pair
\Box_{1}\in\nu_{i}
and
\Box_{2}\in\nu_{j}
form an
attacking inversion
if
\Box_{1}
preceeds
\Box_{2}
in the reading order and
T(\Box_{1})>T(\Box_{2})
. For
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\nu)
, set

\mathrm{inv}(T):=\#\text{attacking inversions of }T.

Definition 6.1
.

The
LLT-polynomial
indexed by a tuple of skew shapes
\nu
is the generating function,

\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(X;t):=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\nu)}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}X^{T}.

It was originally shown in
[
44
]
that the LLT polynomials are symmetric functions. Later, a more elementary proof was provided in
[
33
]
.

Notation:
Given a triangular partition, we have so far chosen to index the associated objects by triples
(m,n,\ell)
. This was relevant given the importance of embedding our triangular partitions inside larger ones. In this section this choice of embedding plays no role, thus in order to facilitate comparisons with the work of the existing combinatorial literature
[
31
,
33
,
34
,
5
,
6
]
, we revert to the initial notation of §
3
and label our triangular partitions and associated objects by tuples of real numbers
(r,s)
where
r=z/n
and
s=z/m
, for some
z
with
\lfloor z\rfloor=\ell
.

So then, for any
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{r,s})\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)
with
{\tilde{r}}=\lfloor r\rfloor+1
, one can associate an
{\tilde{r}}
-tuple of skew-rows
\nu(\pi)
as follows.

Let
\tau_{r,s}^{\prime}=(\tau^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\tau^{\prime}_{{\tilde{r}}-1})
and
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\lambda^{\prime}_{{\tilde{r}}-1})
denote the transpose partitions of
\tau_{r,s}
and
\lambda
respectively. Set:

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}=(\tilde{\tau}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{r}})

\displaystyle:=({\lfloor{s}\rfloor},\tau^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\tau^{\prime}_{{\tilde{r}}-1})

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}=(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{r}})

\displaystyle:=({\lfloor{s}\rfloor},\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\lambda^{\prime}_{{\tilde{r}}-1})

and for each
1\leq i\leq{\tilde{r}}
, let
h_{i}=s-\frac{s}{r}(i-1)-\tilde{\tau}_{i}
and set
\gamma
to be the
{\tilde{r}}
-tuple of skew
rows
with parts,

\gamma_{i}:=(\tilde{\tau}_{i}-\tilde{\lambda}_{i+1})\setminus(\tilde{\tau}_{i}-\tilde{\lambda}_{i})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\gamma_{\tilde{r}}=(\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{r}})\setminus(\tilde{\tau}_{\tilde{r}}-\tilde{\lambda}_{{\tilde{r}}})\qquad\text{ for }\qquad 1\leq i\leq{\tilde{r}}-1,

where
(\ell)
denotes the row partition of size
\ell
.

Let
\sigma
be the permutation in
S_{\tilde{r}}
that sorts
(h_{\tilde{r}},\dots,h_{1})
into an increasing sequence, and define

\nu(\pi):=\sigma\omega_{0}(\gamma)=(\gamma_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,\gamma_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}({\tilde{r}})}),

where
\omega_{0}
is the longest word in
S_{\tilde{r}}
. That is,
\nu(\pi)
is a tuple of (potentially overlapping) skew-rows all at height one obtained by taking the parts
\gamma_{i}
of
\gamma
and sorting them according to the relative size of
h_{i}
.

Remark 6.2
.

Notice that the permutation
\sigma
depends only on the triangular partition
\tau_{r,s}
and the cutting line
L_{r,s},
and not on the choice of subdiagram
\lambda\subseteq\tau_{r,s}
.

Example 6.3
.

Consider
\tau_{4.5,3.6}=(3,2,0)
from Figure
17
and suppose
\lambda=(3,1,0)
. Then,
\tilde{\tau}=(3,2,2,1,0)
and
\tilde{\lambda}=(3,2,1,1,0)
. Hence,
\gamma=((1)\setminus(0),(1)\setminus(0),(1)\setminus(1),(1)\setminus(0),(0)\setminus(0))=((1),(1),(0),(1),(0))
and
h=(0.6,0.8,0,0.2,0.4)
.
Consequently, (in one-line notation)
\sigma=(3\;2\;1\;5\;4)
and
\nu(\pi)=(4\;5\;1\;2\;3)((1),(1),(0),(1),(0))=((0),(1),(0),(1),(1))
.

Blasiak et al. also introduced the rotated tuple
\nu(\pi)^{R}
obtained by taking each skew-row
\nu(\pi)_{i}
, transposing it, then rotating it by
180
degrees, and finally shifting it so that for each box
\Box
in the column the content
c(\Box)
is the same as it was in
\nu(\pi)_{i}=\gamma_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(i)}
. Thus,
\nu(\pi)^{R}
is a
{\tilde{r}}
-tuple of
columns
and
\nu(\pi)
a
{\tilde{r}}
-tuple of
rows
. It follows from the definition that
I(\nu(\pi))=I(\nu(\pi)^{R})
.

The
shuffle theorem under any line

[
6
, Thm. 5.5.1]
gives a combinatorial formula in terms of
\tau
-Dyck paths, with
\tau
a triangular partition, for the action of a family of operators
\mathsf{D}_{\tau}
in the elliptic Hall algebra on the element
1\in\mathbb{C}[q,t][x_{1},x_{2},\dots]^{S_{\infty}}
. Combined with
[
6
, Prop. 4.1.6]
the shuffle theorem states:

\mathsf{D}_{\tau_{r,s}}(1)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\omega(\mathcal{G}_{\nu(\pi)}(X;t^{-1}))=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}t^{-I(\nu(\pi))}\mathcal{G}_{\nu(\pi)^{R}}(X;t),

(6.2)

where
\omega
is the standard Weyl involution on symmetric functions.

Definition 6.4
.

For a subset
J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, Gessel’s
fundamental quasisymmetric function
is given by

Q_{n,J}(X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
a_{i}=a_{i+1}\Rightarrow i\notin J\end{subarray}}x_{a_{1}}x_{a_{2}}\ldots x_{a_{n}}.

(6.3)

It was shown in the Appendix of
[
33
]
that for
S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\nu)
with
descent set

d(S):=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\;|\;S(\Box_{1})=i+1,\;S(\Box_{2})=i\;\text{ and }\Box_{1}\text{ preceeds }\Box_{2}\text{ in reading order}\}

we can write the following:

\mathcal{G}_{\nu}(X;t)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\nu)}q^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}Q_{n,d(S)}(X).

(6.4)

Thus, denoting by
\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t)
the combinatorial side of the shuffle theorem (
6.2
), from (
6.4
) we have,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}t^{-I(\nu(\pi))}\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\nu(\pi)^{R})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}Q_{n,d(S)}(X).

(6.5)

6.2.
Superization

Consider the alphabet
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}_{X}\sqcup\mathsf{A}_{Y}
consisting of two copies of the set of positive integers:
\mathsf{A}_{X}=\{1_{X}<2_{X}<\ldots\}
and
\mathsf{A}_{Y}=\{1_{Y}>2_{Y}>\ldots\}
, ordered so that
k_{X}<k_{Y}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Definition 6.5
.

A
semistandard super tableaux

T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu)
is a filling of
\nu
with entries in
\mathsf{A}
, such that the elements of
\mathsf{A}_{X}
are strictly increasing in columns and the elements of
\mathsf{A}_{Y}
are strictly increasing in rows, with the entries weakly increasing in rows and columns otherwise. Denote by
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu;\mu,\eta)
the set of all semistandard super tableaux of shape
\nu
, with
\mathsf{A}_{X}
-weight equal to
\mu
and
\mathsf{A}_{Y}
-weight equal to
\eta
.

Remark 6.6
.

When
\mu=(a)
and
\eta=(b)
are one part partitions, we will omit the parenthesis and write
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu;a,b)
instead of
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu;(a),(b))
to avoid cumbersome notation.

Two attacking cells
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
in
\nu
form an
attacking inversion
in
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu)
if
\Box_{1}
preceeds
\Box_{2}
in reading order and either
T(\Box_{1})>T(\Box_{2})
or
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})\in\mathsf{A}_{Y}
. As before
\mathrm{inv}(T)
counts the number of attacking inversions of
T
.

In the usual way, to every super tableaux
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\nu;\mu,\eta)
we associate a monomial
Z^{T}:=X^{\mu}Y^{\eta}
. Given any symmetric function
f(X)
, its
superization
is the following function,

\widetilde{f}(Z)=\widetilde{f}(X,Y):=\omega_{Y}f[X+Y],

(6.6)

where
f[X+Y]
denotes the plethystic evaluation of
f
on the formal series
X+Y
and
\omega_{Y}
is Weyl involution on
\mathbb{C}[Y]^{S_{\infty}}
. While we omit all details of plethystic substitution in this article, we refer the reader to
[
32
]
for an introductory treatment of the subject.

The superization of the LLT-polynomial is given by (see the Appendix of
[
33
]
):

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\nu}(Z;t):=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu)}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}Z^{T}.

(6.7)

Similarly, given
J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, the
super fundamental quasisymmetric functions
are defined as,

\widetilde{Q}_{n,J}(Z):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n}\in\mathsf{A}\\
a_{k}=a_{k+1}\in\mathsf{A}_{X}\Rightarrow i\notin J\\
a_{k}=a_{k+1}\in\mathsf{A}_{Y}\Rightarrow i\in J\end{subarray}}z_{a_{1}}z_{a_{2}}\ldots z_{a_{n}}.

(6.8)

In
[
34
, eq. (17)]
, Haglund et al. derive the following identity,

\langle f(X),h_{\mu}e_{\eta}\rangle=\widetilde{f}(X,Y)|_{X^{\mu}Y^{\eta}}.

(6.9)

Hence, in order to compute the right hand side of (
6.1
), we must first compute
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t)
.

Proposition 6.7
.

[
34
, Cor. 2.4.3]

Let
f
be any degree
n
homogeneous symmetric function with expansion into fundamental quasisymmetric functions given by

f(X)=\sum_{J}f_{J}Q_{n,J}(X).

(6.10)

Then, its superization
\widetilde{f}
expands into super fundamental quasisymmetric functions as follows:

\widetilde{f}(Z)=\sum_{J}f_{J}\widetilde{Q}_{n,J}(Z).

(6.11)

Consequently, applying the previous Proposition to both (
6.5
) and (
6.4
), we derive that,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}t^{-I(\nu(\pi))}\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\nu(\pi)^{R})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}\widetilde{Q}_{n,d(S)}(X)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:llt-gessel}}{=}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}t^{-I(\nu(\pi))}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\nu(\pi)^{R}}(Z;t)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:superLLT}}{=}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}t^{-I(\nu(\pi))}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi)^{R})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}Z^{T}.

Thus, from (
6.9
) we have,

\langle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t),h_{\mu}e_{\eta}\rangle=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t)|_{X^{\mu}Y^{\eta}}=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}t^{-I(\nu(\pi))}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi)^{R};\mu,\eta)}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)},

which specialized at
\mu=(k)
and
\eta=({\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k)
yields,

\langle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t),h_{k}e_{{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k}\rangle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi)^{R};k,{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k)}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)-I(\nu(\pi))}.

(6.12)

6.3.
The
(q,t)
-Schröder Theorem

In order to relate equality (
6.12
) to our Schröder polynomials, we need to express it in terms of fillings of
\rho_{\pi}:=(\lambda+1^{{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}})\setminus\lambda
, where
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{r,s})
. To do so, we recall the bijection
[
6
, §6]
:

\displaystyle\Psi:\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi)^{R})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})

(6.13)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto P_{T},

which is obtained by mapping the
i^{th}
component
\nu(\pi)^{R}_{i}
of
\nu(\pi)^{R}
to the
\sigma\omega_{0}(i)^{th}
column of
\rho_{\pi}
and preserving the corresponding labels.

To understand how the associated statistics are modified under this bijection, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 6.8
.

Given a cell
\Box\in\rho_{\pi}
with
northwest
coordinate
(a,b)
, define
\phi(\Box)
to be the vertical distance between
(a,b)
and the line
L_{r,s}
, so that

\phi(\Box):=s-s/r(a)-b.

Definition 6.9
.

We say two cells
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
in
\rho_{\pi}
are an
attacking pair
if
0<|\phi(\Box_{1})-\phi(\Box_{2})|<1
, and denote by
\mathrm{I}(\pi)
the number of attacking pairs in
\rho_{\pi}
.

Similarly, given
P\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})
, we say an attacking pair
\Box_{1},\Box_{2}\in\rho_{\pi}
is an
attacking inversion
if
\phi(\Box_{1})<\phi(\Box_{2})
and either
P(\Box_{1})>P(\Box_{2})
or
P(\Box_{1})=P(\Box_{2})\in\mathsf{A}_{Y}
. Denote by
\mathrm{inv}(P)
the number of attacking inversions of
P
.

Lemma 6.10
.

For any
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi)^{R})
and
P_{T}=\Psi(T)\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})
, we have the following:

\mathrm{I}(\nu(\pi)^{R})=\mathrm{I}(\pi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{inv}(T)=\mathrm{inv}(P_{T}).

Proof.

Since
I(\nu(\pi))=I(\nu(\pi)^{R})
it suffices to consider attacking pairs in
I(\nu(\pi))
.

We begin by noting that from the bijection
6.13
it follows that to any box
\Box
in
\nu(\pi)_{j}
with NE corner
(x,1)
the corresponding box
\widetilde{\Box}
in
\rho_{\pi}
will have
\phi(\widetilde{\Box})=x-1+h_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(j)}
. Moreover, given any two components
\nu_{i}
and
\nu_{j}
then
i<j
if and only if
h_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(i)}<h_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(j)}
.

Let
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
be a pair of boxes in
\nu(\pi)
and let
\widetilde{\Box}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\Box}_{2}
be the corresponding boxes in
\rho_{\pi}.
Let
(x_{1},1)
be the NE corner of
\Box_{1}\in\nu(\pi)_{j_{1}}
and
(x_{2},1)
be the NE corner of
\Box_{2}\in\nu(\pi)_{j_{2}}.
Boxes
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
form an attacking pair if and only if

|c(\Box_{1})-c(\Box_{2})|=|(x_{1}-x_{2})+(j_{1}-j_{2})\epsilon|<1,

while boxes
\widetilde{\Box}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\Box}_{2}
for an attacking pair if and only if

|\phi(\widetilde{\Box}_{1})-\phi(\widetilde{\Box}_{2})|=|(x_{1}-x_{2})+(h_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(j_{1})}-h_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(j_{2})})|<1.

To show that
\mathrm{I}(\nu(\pi)^{R})=\mathrm{I}(\pi)
it remains to notice that the two conditions are equivalent. Indeed,
(x_{1}-x_{2})
is an integer, and the correction terms
(j_{1}-j_{2})\epsilon
and
(h_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(j_{1})}-h_{\omega_{0}\sigma^{-1}(j_{2})})
always have the same sign and are less than
1
by absolute value.

For the second claim we recall the bijection in Proposition 4.1.6 in
[
6
]
:

\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi))\to\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi)^{R})\qquad\qquad U\mapsto U^{R}

obtained by reflecting a tableau and exchanging positive entries in
\mathsf{A}_{X}
with negative entries
\mathsf{A}_{Y}
. In particular, Blasiak et. al. show that under this bijection
\mathrm{inv}(U^{R})=I(\nu(\pi))-\mathrm{inv}(U)
. Thus, it suffices to prove that attacking inversions in
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})
correspond to attacking non-inversions of
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu(\pi))
.

So then, given a pair of attacking cells
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
in
\nu(\pi)
notice that the attacking condition ensures that
c(\Box_{1})\neq c(\Box_{2})
. Hence, these cells form an attacking non-inversion if
c(\Box_{1})<c(\Box_{2})
with either
U(\Box_{1})<U(\Box_{2})
or
U(\Box_{1})=U(\Box_{2})\in\mathsf{A}_{X}
. Since by definition the alphabets are ordered such that
\{1_{X}<2_{X}<\dots\}
and
\{1_{Y}>2_{Y}>\dots\}
with
k_{X}<k_{Y}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then under under
\Psi
, these conditions correspond to
\phi(\widetilde{\Box}_{1})<\phi(\widetilde{\Box}_{2})
with either
P_{U^{R}}(\widetilde{\Box}_{1})>P_{U^{R}}(\widetilde{\Box}_{2})
or
P_{U^{R}}(\widetilde{\Box}_{1})=P_{U^{R}}(\widetilde{\Box}_{2})\in\mathsf{A}_{Y}
, as desired.

∎

Thus, in light of Lemma
6.10
, we may rewrite (
6.12
) as follows:

\langle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t),h_{k}e_{{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k}\rangle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\sum_{P\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi};k,{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k)}t^{\mathrm{inv}(P)-I(\pi)}.

(6.14)

Now, recall from (
4.6
) that

\displaystyle S_{\tau_{r,s}}(q,t,a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\sum_{L\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)}a^{|L|}t^{-\sum_{\Box\in L}\xi(\pi,\Box)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}a^{k}\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)\\
|L|=k\end{subarray}}t^{-\sum_{\Box\in L}\xi(\pi,\Box)}\right).

Thus, (
6.1
) is an immediate consequence of the subsequent result.

Theorem 6.11
.

The following equality holds:

\sum_{P\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi};k,{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k)}t^{\mathrm{inv}(P)-I(\pi)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)\\
|L|=k\end{subarray}}t^{-\sum_{\Box\in L}\xi(\pi,\Box)}.

Proof.

Consider any
P\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi};k,{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k)
. Then by definition, since entries in
\mathsf{A}_{X}
are strictly increasing on columns and
P
has exactly
k
boxes with entries
1_{X}
and
{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k
boxes with entries
1_{Y}
, then any column of
\rho_{\pi}
can have at most one cell with value
1_{X}
. Thus, any choice of
P\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi};k,{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k)
is equivalent to a choice of
k
columns of
\rho_{\pi}
on which the bottom-most entry is filled with
1_{X}
, with all remaining entries filled with
1_{Y}
. Evidently then, since the set bottom-most cells in
\rho_{\pi}
are precisely the addable boxes of
\lambda
, there is an obvious bijection between

\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi};k,{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k)\to\mathcal{P}(\mathtt{AB}(\pi))

that maps each tableaux
P
to the subset
L_{P}
of boxes of
\rho_{\pi}
labeled by
1_{X}
in
P
.

Thus, it remains to show that for any such
P
we have
I(\pi)-\mathrm{inv}(P)=\sum_{\Box\in L_{P}}\xi(\pi,\Box)
. Given the restriction on the fillings it follows that the set attacking non-inversions of
P
corresponds to attacking cells
\Box_{1},\Box_{2}\in\rho_{\pi}
satisfying
\phi(\Box_{1})<\phi(\Box_{2})
with
P(\Box_{1})=1_{X}
.

Fix a
P\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})
and recall from Definition
4.25
that for any positive integers
m
and
n>s
with
n/m=s/r
if
\Box_{1}\in L_{P}\subset\mathtt{AB}(\pi)
, we have,

\xi(\pi,\Box_{1})=\left|\{\Box\in\mathtt{EB}(\pi)\;|\;\gamma(\Box_{1})+n<\gamma(\Box)\leq\gamma(\Box_{1})+n+m\}\right|.

However, since the cells of
\rho_{\pi}
are in bijection with those in
\mathtt{EB}(\pi)
, i.e. for every cell
\Box\in\mathtt{EB}(\pi)
with NE corner
(a,b)
there is a cell
\Box^{\prime}\in\rho_{\pi}
with NW corner
(a,b)
satisfying
\gamma(\Box)=\gamma(\Box^{\prime})+n
. Then,

\xi(\pi,\Box_{1})=\left|\{\Box_{2}\in\rho_{\pi}\;|\;\gamma(\Box_{1})<\gamma(\Box_{2})\leq\gamma(\Box_{1})+m\}\right|.

So, suppose
\Box_{1}\in L_{P}
with NW corner
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
\Box_{2}\in\rho_{\pi}
with NW corner
(x_{2},y_{2})
. Then, if
\Box_{2}\in\rho_{\pi}
satisfies
\gamma(\Box_{1})<\gamma(\Box_{2})\leq\gamma(\Box_{1})+m
,
we have the following:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}mn-my_{1}-n(x_{1}+1)&<&mn-my_{2}-n(x_{2}+1)&\leq&mn-my_{1}-n(x_{1}+1)+m\\
-my_{1}-nx_{1}&<&-my_{2}-nx_{2}&\leq&-my_{1}-nx_{1}+m\\
0&<&m(y_{1}-y_{2})+n(x_{1}-x_{2})&\leq&m\\
0&<&(y_{1}-y_{2})+\frac{n}{m}(x_{1}-x_{2})&\leq&1\\
0&<&\phi(\Box_{2})-\phi(\Box_{1})&\leq&1.\end{array}

Note that the equality on the right can never occur. Indeed, since
n
and
m
are relatively prime and
|x_{1}-x_{2}|<m,
that would require
x_{1}=x_{2}
and
y_{1}=y_{2}+1,
which means that
\Box_{1}
lies directly on top of
\Box_{2},
but
\Box_{2}\in\rho_{\pi}
and
\Box_{1}
is an addable box. Thus,
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
are attacking with
\phi(\Box_{1})<\phi(\Box_{2})
. Since by construction
P(\Box_{1})=1_{X}
, this completes the proof.
∎

Consequently, we immediately obtain a
(q,t)
-Schröder Theorem for paths under any line.

Corollary 6.12
.

The triangular
(q,t)
-Schröder polynomial computes the hook components of the shuffle theorem for paths under any line. That is,

S_{\tau_{r,s}}(q,t,a)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\langle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t),h_{k}e_{{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k}\rangle\;a^{k}.

In particular, since
\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t)
depends solely on the choice of triangular partition and not the choice of slope, Corollary
6.12
once again implies that the triangular Schröder polynomial depends only on
\tau
.

Appendix A
An Alternative Derivation of the Hook Component

In this section we provide an alternative derivation of (
6.14
) where the superization procedure is performed after the bijection from (
6.13
) has been applied, rather than before. This allows us to provide a self contained argument that does not rely on the proofs of
[
34
]
or the appendix in
[
33
]
.

Given a triangular partition
\tau=\tau_{r,s}
with
\pi=(\lambda,\tau_{r,s})\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)
and
\rho_{\pi}=\left(\lambda+1^{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}\right)/\lambda
, let
\mathrm{inv}(T)
with
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})
be given as in Definition
6.9
.

Definition A.1
.

The LLT polynomial corresponding to
\rho_{\pi}=\left(\lambda+1^{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}\right)/\lambda
is given by the formula,

\mathcal{L}_{\pi}(X,t):=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\lambda})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}X^{T}.

(A.1)

It follows from Lemma
6.10
that
\mathcal{L}_{\pi}(X,t)=\mathcal{G}_{\nu(\pi)^{R}}(X;t)
. Thus, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t):=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)}\omega(\mathcal{G}_{\nu(\pi)}(X;t^{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\nu(\pi))}\mathcal{G}_{\nu(\pi)^{R}}(X;t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\pi)}\mathcal{L}_{\pi}(X;t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\pi)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}X^{T}.

Remark A.2
.

In the special case when
r=n
is an integer and
s=n+\epsilon
, one obtains that
\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)=I(\pi)
, for all
\pi\in\mathrm{D}(n,n+\epsilon)
and the formula above specializes into the right hand side of the classical Shuffle Theorem (see Formula (29) in
[
34
]
and
[
9
]
).

A.1.
Superization

Our goal now is to evaluate the Hall scalar product
\langle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X,q,t),h_{k}e_{{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}-k}\rangle
using the formula above and the superization methods from
[
34
]
.

The Pieri rule immediately implies that for a triple of partitions
\lambda,\mu,\eta
, one obtains

\langle s_{\lambda},h_{\mu}e_{\eta}\rangle=|\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\lambda;\mu,\eta)|.

(A.2)

Lemma A.3
.

For any three symmetric functions
f_{1},\ f_{2},
and
f_{3}
, one has

\langle f_{1},f_{2}f_{3}\rangle=\langle\langle f_{1}[X+Y],f_{2}[X]\rangle_{X},f_{3}[Y]\rangle_{Y}.

Proof.

Both sides are linear with respect to all three functions, hence it is enough to check the equation for
f_{1}=m_{\lambda},

f_{2}=h_{\mu},
and
f_{3}=h_{\eta}.
Then for the left hand side, one gets

\langle m_{\lambda},h_{\mu}h_{\eta}\rangle=\langle m_{\lambda},h_{\mu\sqcup\eta}\rangle=\delta_{\lambda,\mu\sqcup\eta},

where
\mu\sqcup\eta:=\text{sort}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\ldots,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots),

while for the right hand side, one also gets

\left\langle\langle m_{\lambda}[X+Y],h_{\mu}[X]\rangle_{X},h_{\eta}[Y]\right\rangle_{Y}=\left\langle\sum_{\nu\sqcup\mu=\lambda}m_{\nu}[Y],h_{\eta}[Y]\right\rangle_{Y}=\delta_{\lambda,\mu\sqcup\eta}.

Hence, the statement holds.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

For any triple of partitions
\lambda,\mu,\eta
, the following holds:

\langle f,h_{\mu}e_{\eta}\rangle=\widetilde{f}(X,Y)|_{X^{\mu}Y^{\eta}}.

(A.3)

Proof.

Indeed,

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(X,Y)|_{X^{\mu}Y^{\eta}}

\displaystyle=\langle\langle\omega_{Y}f[X+Y],h_{\mu}[X]\rangle_{X},h_{\eta}[Y]\rangle_{Y}

\displaystyle=\langle\omega_{Y}\langle f[X+Y],h_{\mu}[X]\rangle_{X},h_{\eta}[Y]\rangle_{Y}

\displaystyle=\langle\langle f[X+Y],h_{\mu}[X]\rangle_{X},e_{\eta}[Y]\rangle_{Y}

\displaystyle=\langle f,h_{\mu}e_{\eta}\rangle,

where on the last step we use Lemma
A.3
.
∎

Corollary A.5
.

Formula
A.2
and Lemma
A.4
imply

\tilde{s}_{\lambda}(Z)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\lambda)}Z^{T}.

(A.4)

A.2.
Quasisymmetric Functions

Recall Gessel’s fundamental quasisymmetric functions from Definition
6.4
. It is well known that Schur functions can be expressed in terms of these functions.

Definition A.6
.

Let
S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)
. For every
k\in\{1,\ldots n\}
, let
\Box_{k}=(x_{k},y_{k})\in\lambda
be the box of
\lambda
such that
S(\Box_{k})=k.
We say that
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
is a
right step
if
\Box_{k+1}
is to the right of
\Box_{k}
(i.e.
x_{k+1}>x_{k}
), and we say that
k
is an
up step
if
\Box_{k+1}
is higher than
\Box_{k}
(i.e.
y_{k+1}>y_{k}
). Note that every
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
is either a right step, or an up step, but not both. Then,

\{1,\ldots,n-1\}=R(S)\sqcup U(S),

where
R(S)
(resp.
U(S)
) is the set of right (resp. up) steps.

Lemma A.7

(
[
21
]
, Theorem
7
)
.

For any partition
\lambda
,

s_{\lambda}(X)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)}Q_{|\lambda|,R(S)}(X).

We will prove Lemma
A.7
, as we will need a modification of this argument later on.

Proof.

The
standardization map

\mathrm{St}:\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda)\to\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)
is constructed as follows. Let
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda)
be a semistandard tableau. Then the standard tableau
S:=\mathrm{St}(T)
is defined by the following conditions:

(1)

If
T(\Box_{1})<T(\Box_{2})
, then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}),

(2)

If
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})
and
\Box_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})
is to the left of
\Box_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})
(i.e.
x_{1}<x_{2}
), then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}).

Indeed, since if
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})
then
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
cannot be in the same column, the above conditions determine a total order on the values of
S
on the squares of
\lambda,
such that the values in the rows increase left to right and values in the columns increase down to up, which uniquely determines the tableau
S=\mathrm{St}(T)\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda).

Let us reverse the construction in order to understand the fiber
\mathrm{St}^{-1}(S)\subset\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda).
As before, let

\Box_{1},\ldots,\Box_{n}
be the boxes of
\lambda
such that
S(\Box_{k})=k
, for all
k.
Then
T^{\prime}\in\mathrm{St}^{-1}(S)
if and only if for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, and therefore,

(1)

T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})\leq T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1}),

(2)

If
T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})=T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1})
then
k\in R(S).

Hence

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(X)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda)}X^{T}=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{St}^{-1}(S)}X^{T}=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)}Q_{|\lambda|,R(S)}(X).

∎

Now, recall the super quasi-symmetric functions
\tilde{Q}_{n,J}(Z)
from Definition (
6.8
). In
[
6
]
, the following generalization of Lemma
A.7
is proven.

Lemma A.8

(
[
6
]
, Proposition
2.4.2
)
.

One can express the superization of Schur functions in terms of the superization of the Gessel’s functions:

\tilde{s}_{\lambda}(Z)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)}\tilde{Q}_{|\lambda|,R(S)}(Z).

(A.5)

One can prove Lemma
A.8
by extending the standardization map
\mathrm{St}:\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda)\to\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)
to the set of super Young tableaux
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\lambda)
, as we now explain.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\lambda)
be a super Young tableau. Then the standard tableau
S:=\mathrm{St}_{\pm}(T)
is defined by the conditions:

(1)

If
T(\Box_{1})<T(\Box_{2})
, then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}),

(2)

If
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})\in\mathsf{A}_{X}
and
\Box_{1}
is to the left of
\Box_{2}
, then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}).

(3)

If
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})\in\mathsf{A}_{Y}
and
\Box_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})
is lower than
\Box_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})
(i.e.
y_{1}<y_{2}
), then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}).

Similarly to before, these conditions uniquely determine the standard tableau
S:=\mathrm{St}_{\pm}(T).
Furthermore, the fibers of the map
\mathrm{St}_{\pm}:\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\lambda)\to\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)
can be described as follows. Hence,
T^{\prime}\in\mathrm{St}_{\pm}(\lambda)
if and only if for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
and

(1)

T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})\leq T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1}),

(2)

If
T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})=T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1})\in\mathsf{A}_{X}
, then
k\in R(S),

(3)

If
T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})=T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1})\in\mathsf{A}_{Y}
, then
k\in U(S).

Hence

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{\lambda}(Z)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\lambda)}Z^{T}=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{St}_{\pm}^{-1}(S)}Z^{T}=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\lambda)}\tilde{Q}_{|\lambda|,R(S)}(Z).

Extending Lemmas
A.7
and
A.8
by linearity, we obtain Proposition
6.7
.

The proofs of Lemmas
A.7
and
A.8
also work for skew Schur functions, so that for any skew shape
\nu=\lambda/\mu
, the following equalities hold:

s_{\nu}(X)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\nu)}X^{T}=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\nu)}Q_{n,R(S)}(X),

(A.6)

and

\tilde{s}_{\nu}(Z)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\nu)}\widetilde{Q}_{n,R(S)}(Z)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\nu)}Z^{T},

(A.7)

where for the first step we use Proposition
6.7
.

A.3.
Standardization of LLT polynomials

Following
[
34
]
, it is now our goal to apply the standardization construction to the polynomials
\mathcal{L}_{\pi}(X,t)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}X^{T}
and express them as linear combinations of Gessel’s quasi-symmetric functions. The challenge is to modify the standardization map
\mathrm{St}:\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})\to\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\lambda})
so that it preserves the
\mathrm{inv}
statistic. Luckily, the skew shape
\rho_{\pi}
is a vertical strip, which enables us to do the required modifications.

Consider the map
\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}:\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})\to\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})
defined as follows: Given
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})
, its standardization
S:=\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}(T)
is uniquely defined by the conditions:

(1)

If
T(\Box_{1})<T(\Box_{2})
, then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}),

(2)

If
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})
and
\phi(\Box_{1})<\phi(\Box_{2})
, then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}),

where
\phi
is given as in Definition
6.8
.

Note that the condition on the slope implies that
\phi(\Box_{1})\neq\phi(\Box_{2})
for any two distinct squares of
\rho_{\pi},
therefore, the above conditions define a total order on the values of
S
on the boxes of
\rho_{\pi}.
Since
\rho_{\pi}
is a vertical strip, to show that this order defines a standard tableau, one only needs to check that it is increasing in columns down to up. But
T
is strictly increasing in columns, hence by condition (
1
) so does
S
.

Reversing the construction, one gets that for a standard tableau
S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})
, the preimage
\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}^{-1}(S)\subset\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})
can be described as follows. Let
\Box_{1},\ldots,\Box_{n}
be the boxes of
\rho_{\pi}
such that
S(\Box_{k})=k
, for all
k.
Then
T^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}^{-1}(S)
if and only if for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}

(1)

T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})\leq T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1})
and

(2)

If
T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})=T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1})
then
\phi(\Box_{k})<\phi(\Box_{k+1}).

Definition A.9
.

The set
d_{\phi}(S)
of
\phi
-descents
of a standard tableau
S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})
is given by

d_{\phi}(S):=\left\{k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}|\phi(\Box_{k})>\phi(\Box_{k+1})\right\}.

Lemma A.10
.

For any semistandard tableau
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})
one gets

\mathrm{inv}(T)=\mathrm{inv}(\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}(T)).

Proof.

Denote
S:=\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}(T).
Let
(\Box_{1},\Box_{2})
be an attacking pair of boxes of
\rho_{\pi}
(i.e.
|\phi(\Box_{1})-\phi(\Box_{2})|<1
). Without loss of generality, assume that
\phi(\Box_{1})>\phi(\Box_{2}).
The pair
(\Box_{1},\Box_{2})
forms an attacking inversion of
T
if and only if
T(\Box_{1})<T(\Box_{2}),
in which case one also has
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}),
so the pair
(\Box_{1},\Box_{2})
also forms an attacking inversion of
S.

Conversely, the pair
(\Box_{1},\Box_{2})
forms an attacking inversion of
S
if and only if
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}).
Since
\phi(\Box_{1})>\phi(\Box_{2}),
this implies that
T(\Box_{1})<T(\Box_{2}),
so the pair
(\Box_{1},\Box_{2})
also forms an attacking inversion for
T.

We conclude that the attacking inversions of
T
and of
S=\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}(T)
are in bijection, hence
\mathrm{inv}(T)=\mathrm{inv}(\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}(T)).

∎

Lemma
A.10
and the above construction imply

Lemma A.11
.

For any
\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)
,

\mathcal{L}_{\pi}(X,t)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}Q_{n,d_{\phi}(S)}(X).

Applying Proposition
6.7
, we also obtain expansions for the superizations of the LLT polynomials:

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi}(Z,t)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}\tilde{Q}_{n,d_{\phi}(S)}(Z).

(A.8)

Now, consider an extension of the map
\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}:\mathrm{SSYT}(\rho_{\pi})\to\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})
to the set
\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})
of super tableaux, defined as follows. Given a super tableau
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})
, its standardization
S:=\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}_{\pm}(T)\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})
is uniquely determined by the following conditions:

(1)

If
T(\Box_{1})<T(\Box_{2})
, then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}),

(2)

If
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{X}
and
\phi(\Box_{1})<\phi(\Box_{2})
, then
S(\Box_{1})<S(\Box_{2}),

(3)

If
T(\Box_{1})=T(\Box_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}
and
\phi(\Box_{1})<\phi(\Box_{2})
, then
S(\Box_{1})>S(\Box_{2}).

Similar to before, these conditions uniquely define the standard tableau
S=\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}_{\pm}(T).
Furthermore, one can reverse the construction to compute the fiber
\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}_{\pm}^{-1}(S).
As before, let
\Box_{1},\ldots,\Box_{n}
be the boxes of
\rho_{\pi},
such that
S(\Box_{k})=k
for all
k.
Then
T^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}_{\pm}^{-1}(S)
if and only if for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, and

(1)

T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})\leq T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1}),

(2)

If
T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})=T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1})\in\mathcal{A}_{X}
, then
\phi(\Box_{k})<\phi(\Box_{k+1}),
and

(3)

If
T^{\prime}(\Box_{k})=T^{\prime}(\Box_{k+1})\in\mathcal{A}_{Y}
, then
\phi(\Box_{k})>\phi(\Box_{k+1}).

In particular, for any
S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})
, it follows that

\sum_{T\in\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}_{\pm}^{-1}(S)}Z^{T}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n}\in\mathsf{A}\\
a_{i}=a_{i+1}\in\mathsf{A}_{+}\Rightarrow\phi(\Box_{k})<\phi(\Box_{k+1})\\
a_{i}=a_{i+1}\in\mathsf{A}_{-}\Rightarrow\phi(\Box_{k})<\phi(\Box_{k+1})\end{subarray}}Z^{T}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\leq\ldots\leq a_{n}\in\mathsf{A}\\
a_{i}=a_{i+1}\in\mathsf{A}_{+}\Rightarrow k\notin d_{\phi}(S)\\
a_{i}=a_{i+1}\in\mathsf{A}_{-}\Rightarrow k\in d_{\phi}(S)\end{subarray}}Z^{T}=\widetilde{Q}_{n,d_{\phi}(S)}(Z).

The definition of
\mathrm{inv}
statistic can be extended to the super tableaux using
\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}_{\pm}:

Definition A.12
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})
and set

\mathrm{inv}(T):=\mathrm{inv}(\widehat{\mathrm{{St}}}_{\pm}(T)).

Then the following holds:

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi}(Z,t)=\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}\tilde{Q}_{n,d_{\phi}(S)}(Z)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}Z^{T}.

Now, recall the notion of attacking inversions from Definition
6.9
. Applying Lemma
A.11
to the RHS of the shuffle theorem under any line one implies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\pi)}\mathcal{L}_{\pi}(X;t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\pi)}\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}Q_{n,d_{\phi}(S)}(X).

Applying Proposition
6.7
and (
6.8
), one can extend these formula to the superizations.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau_{r,s}}(Z;q,t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\pi)}\sum_{S\in\mathrm{SYT}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(S)}\widetilde{Q}_{n,D_{\phi}(S)}(Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\pi)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi})}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}Z^{T}.

We now can apply Lemma
A.4
to get the following formula:

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X;q,t),h_{\mu}e_{\eta}\rangle=

\displaystyle\;\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau_{r,s}}(X,Y;q,t)|_{X^{\mu}Y^{\eta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{D}(r,s)}q^{\mathtt{area}(\pi)}t^{\mathtt{dinv}(\pi)-I(\pi)}\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}_{\pm}(\rho_{\pi};\mu,\eta)}t^{\mathrm{inv}(T)}.

Specializing, the result now follows.

Appendix B
Sequences for Links

Let
a
and
b
be positive relatively prime integers, and
g
be an integer greater than
1.
Consider
\tau=\tau_{ga,gb}.
Let also
c
and
d
be the unique positive integers such that
c\leq a,

d\leq b,
and
ad-bc=1.
Set
m:=ga+c,

n:=gb+d,
and
\ell=mn-dn-1=(gb+d)ga-1.

Finally, set
(r,s):=(\ell/n,\ell/m)=\left(\frac{(gb+d)ga-1}{gb+d},\frac{(gb+d)ga-1}{ga+c}\right).

Lemma B.1
.

In the notations as above one obtains

(1)

\tau_{r,s}=\tau=\tau_{ga,gb},

(2)

{\bf u}(m,n,\ell)=0^{g-1}10^{g(a-1)}
and
{\bf v}(m,n,\ell)=0^{gb}1.

Proof.

According to Lemma
5.15
one has

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }i\equiv(gb+d)ga-1\text{ mod }(ga+c)\\
0&\text{ if }i\equiv(gb+d)ga-(gb+d)x-1\text{ mod }(ga+c)\text{ for }x\in\left\{1,\ldots,\left\lfloor\frac{(gb+d)ga-1}{gb+d}\right\rfloor\right\}\\
\bullet&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle y_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }j\equiv(gb+d)ga-1\text{ mod }(gb+d)\\
0&\text{ if }j\equiv(gb+d)ga-(ga+c)y-1\text{ mod }(gb+d)\text{ for }y\in\left\{1,\ldots,\left\lfloor\frac{(gb+d)ga-1}{ga+c}\right\rfloor\right\}\\
\bullet&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Note that
gb<\frac{(gb+d)ga-1}{ga+c}<gb+1.
Indeed, cross-multiplying and using
ad-bc=1
one gets

g^{2}ab+gda-1-g^{2}ab-gbc=g-1>0,

for the left inequality, and

g^{2}ab+gbc+ga+c-g^{2}ab-gad+1=g(a-1)+c+1>0

for the right inequality. Note also that

(gb+d)a=gab+ad=gab+bc+1=b(ga+c)+1\equiv 1\text{ mod }ga+c.

Using this and simplifying, one gets

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }i\equiv g-1\text{ mod }(ga+c)\\
0&\text{ if }i\equiv g-(gb+d)x-1\text{ mod }(ga+c)\text{ for }x\in\{1,\ldots,ga-1\}\\
\bullet&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle y_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }j\equiv-1\text{ mod }(gb+d)\\
0&\text{ if }j\equiv(gb+d)ga-(ga+c)y-1\text{ mod }(gb+d)\text{ for }y\in\{1,\ldots,gb\}\\
\bullet&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Taking
x=ka
with
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,g-1\}
it follows that
g-(gb+d)ak-1\equiv g-k-1
mod
(ga+c).
One concludes that
x_{0}=x_{1}=\ldots=x_{g-2}=0,

x_{g-1}=1,
and there are
ga-1
zeros in
{\bf x}.
Hence
{\bf u}=0^{g-1}10^{g(a-1)}.

Also, there are
gb
zeros in
{\bf y}
and the its last entry is
1.
Hence
{\bf v}=0^{gb}1.

∎
